\subsection{Enclosures far beyond the range of a power-series evaluation}
Equation \eqref{gml:eq:Uinverse} has a useful exact numerical consequence.
Choose $s_0>0$, set $s_{-1}=U(s_0)$, and define
\begin{equation}\label{gml:eq:orbit}
 s_{k+1}=s_k(1+s_{k-1})\qquad(k\ge0).
\end{equation}
Then $U(s_k)=s_{k-1}$ for every $k\ge0$. Indeed,
$U^{-1}(s_k)=s_k(1+U(s_k))=s_k(1+s_{k-1})$.
Thus values of the true transform can be transported along its inverse
orbit without any approximation to the functional equation.

For a rigorous seed, take $s_0=1/100$. Equation \eqref{gml:eq:remainder} gives
\begin{equation}\label{gml:eq:seedbounds}
 \sum_{j=0}^{31}(-1)^ja_js_0^j\le F(s_0)
 \le\sum_{j=0}^{30}(-1)^ja_js_0^j.
\end{equation}
Both endpoints are rational and computed exactly. Positive additions and
multiplications preserve lower and upper enclosures under
\eqref{gml:eq:orbit}. The script \texttt{code/02-golden-moments-orbit\_bounds.py} propagates
these enclosures with 100-digit Decimal arithmetic, rounding down for
lower endpoints and up for upper endpoints. Correctly rounded logarithms,
square roots and exponentials are enlarged by one adjacent representable
value on each side before they are used in an enclosure \cite{decimal}.
Subtracted products are rounded in the opposite direction before subtraction.

For each row in the following table there is a true inverse-orbit point
$s_k$ in the full logarithmic interval recorded in
\texttt{data/02-golden-moments-orbit\_bounds.json}. The middle column is only a rounded
location; the last column is a widened outward enclosure of
\[
 s_k^\alpha F(s_k)=\frac{s_{k-1}}{s_k^\beta}.
\]
The enclosure concerns that true point, not every point in the interval.
\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabular}{r r l}
\toprule
$k$&approximate $\log s_k$&enclosure of $s_k^\alpha F(s_k)$\\
\midrule
\tableinput{data/02-golden-moments-orbit_table.tex}
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
These are finite computational enclosures conditional only on the exact
identities proved above and the correctly rounded arithmetic operations.
They illustrate the constant-one law at extremely large arguments. They
are not its proof: Lemma~\ref{gml:lem:rigidity} proves that law at all sufficiently
large arguments, not merely along the selected orbit.

\subsection{Reproduction and complexity}
In the delivered package layout, where the three scripts and their data
files carry no prefix, the checks and the delivered three-pass build were
\begin{quote}\ttfamily\small
python3 code/verify.py --n 401\\
python3 code/orbit\_bounds.py\\
python3 code/make\_tables.py\\
sh build.sh
\end{quote}
The scripts write their outputs under those unprefixed names, not over the
shipped files with the prefix \texttt{02-golden-moments-}; the report's
README gives the commands for rerunning them on a copy. The PDF of this
report is built by its \texttt{Makefile}.
The coefficient generator uses
$N(N+1)(N+2)/6$ integer products and $O(N^2)$ integer storage cells.
These are arithmetic-operation counts, not unit-cost bit-complexity
claims: the coefficients grow rapidly. The small formal-series, Fock,
and determinant checks use independent formulas to reduce the chance of
an indexing or recurrence error. The PDF build requires a normal
pdfLaTeX installation and the packages listed in the preamble; the
mathematical checks do not require a computer algebra system.

\section{Further research questions and concrete next steps}\label{gml:sec:research}
The moment representation changes the available methods: it allows
orthogonal polynomials, resolvent bounds, probability tilting, and
Tauberian arguments to be used alongside formal reversion. The following
questions are not treated as established consequences of finite data.
They are follow-up tasks for this construction, not claims that every item
is a previously recognized open problem; existing general theorems may
resolve some of them once their hypotheses are checked.

\begin{question}[Regularity and the support of the two laws]
Are $\mu$ and $\nu$ absolutely continuous on $(0,\infty)$? Do their supports
fill all of $[0,\infty)$, or can gaps or positive atoms occur?
\end{question}
The present proof gives unbounded support, no atom at zero, and precise
mass near zero, but none of these excludes gaps or a singular component.
A concrete first step is to check applicable free-convolution regularity
theorems, and then continue the coupled Cauchy-transform identities
into the upper half-plane and analyze boundary values. Any proposed
density asymptotic must include a regularity argument; differentiating
\eqref{gml:eq:CDF} is not sufficient.
[Batch 86, 4 October 2026.] Answered in Part~IV: both laws are absolutely continuous,
with strictly positive real-analytic densities on $(0,\infty)$ and
supports $[0,\infty)$, so there are no gaps and no atoms; the densities
have explicit equivalents at zero (Theorem~\ref{gdy:reg:thm:main}), as do
their derivatives of every fixed order
(Corollary~\ref{gdy:reg:cor:derivatives}). The proof continues the
transform identities into the upper half-plane and does not differentiate
\eqref{gml:eq:CDF}.

\begin{question}[A full asymptotic equivalent for the coefficients]
What is the multiplicative asymptotic equivalent of $a_n$? In particular,
does the finer Bell-number normalization proposed in Part~I
(Conjecture~\ref{conj:finer}) converge to a finite positive constant?
\end{question}
Part~I proposes a correction involving
$\exp(W(n)^2+3W(n))$ relative to Bell numbers. The ratio law proved here
is weaker: an error tending to zero in a single ratio can still accumulate
into an unbounded logarithmic error. A practical next step is to derive a
uniform expansion of the tilted-law mean beyond $n/\log n$, with a
summable remainder after the proposed normalization.
[Batch 76, 2 October 2026.] Part~III makes partial progress and does not
answer the question. It proves
$\liminf_{n\to\infty}(a_n/a_{n-1}-n/W(n))\ge3/2$, where the proposed
normalization needs the correction $2$ (Theorem~\ref{ble:thm:lower}),
reduces convergence of the normalization to the single scalar estimate
\eqref{ble:eq:defect} (Theorem~\ref{ble:thm:criterion}), and shows that a
quantitative relative-variance estimate for the tilted law suffices for
its curvature half (Proposition~\ref{ble:prop:variance}).
[Batch 86, 4 October 2026.] Proved in Part~V (batch~86, manuscript~02), not yet
independently reviewed: the finer normalization converges,
$a_n=C_*\mathcal N_n(1+O(W(n)^5/n))$ and
$a_n\sim C_*\mathsf B_n\exp(W(n)^2+3W(n))$ with $0<C_*<\infty$
(Theorem~\ref{bnc:thm:bell}). The value of $C_*$ and the ratio expansion
\eqref{eq:neededratio} remain open (Research questions~\ref{bnc:q:constant}
and~\ref{bnc:q:ratio}). Part~IV, from manuscript~01 of the same batch,
which was written without knowledge of manuscript~02, still calls the
question open.

\begin{question}[Second-order golden-ratio asymptotics]
Can $F(s)=s^{-\alpha}(1+o(1))$ be sharpened to an explicit second-order
expansion, and does its correction exhibit oscillation under the inverse
iteration $s_{k+1}=s_k(1+s_{k-1})$?
\end{question}
The exact equation
$d(x)=-\beta d(g(x))-\beta\eps(x)$ is a suitable starting point.
Its negative contraction coefficient makes orbit-dependent alternating
corrections plausible, but the present proof does not establish a global
oscillatory profile or an optimal error rate.
[Batch 86, 4 October 2026.] Answered in Part~IV: Theorem~\ref{gdy:dyn:thm:allorders}
expands $\log(s^\alpha F(s))$ in powers of $1/\log s$, with coefficients
built from a nonzero real-analytic profile $P$ of $\log_\varphi\log s$
satisfying $P(t+1)=-P(t)$, and Corollary~\ref{gdy:dyn:cor:sharp} shows
that the correction oscillates: $(\log s)(s^\alpha F(s)-1)$ has upper and
lower limits $\pm M$ with $M>0.00095$, and alternates in sign along the
inverse orbit. The equation for $d(x)$ above is the starting point there,
in the form \eqref{gdy:dyn:eq:stable}.

\begin{question}[Effective convergence of the certified approximants]
How quickly do $F_{2r}(s)-F_{2r+1}(s)$ tend to zero, uniformly when $s$
varies over a growing interval? Can one obtain practical a priori
stopping bounds for numerical evaluation?
\end{question}
The proof gives monotone convergence on fixed compact intervals, but
not a useful global complexity estimate. The known finite-stage Fibonacci
exponents indicate why the limits $r\to\infty$ and $s\to\infty$ require
care. Interval evaluation with adaptive algebraic root isolation is a
concrete computational approach.

\begin{question}[Orthogonal polynomials and continued fractions]
What are the asymptotics of the Jacobi or Stieltjes continued-fraction
coefficients associated with $\mu$ and $\nu$?
\end{question}
Strict Hankel positivity guarantees nondegeneracy of every finite
orthogonalization step, while \eqref{gml:eq:detbridge} ties the two systems
together. Exact determinant tables can generate candidate recurrence
coefficients, but the main challenge is to prove their large-degree
behavior and connect it to the two endpoint laws.

\begin{question}[Sharper comparison of the companion sequence]
Does $c_n/a_{n-1}\to1$, and what is the first correction if it does?
\end{question}
Proposition~\ref{gml:prop:companion} supplies only a polynomial-factor bound.
The renewal recurrence suggests that endpoint terms should dominate its
convolutions. Turning that observation into a uniform bound on the entire
convolution, rather than on a fixed number of terms, would resolve the
question without assuming the full asymptotics of $a_n$.
[Batch 86, 4 October 2026.] Answered in Part~IV, exactly along these lines:
$c_n=\sum_{j=0}^{K}\gamma_ja_{n-1-j}+O_K(a_{n-K-2})$ for every fixed $K$,
with $\gamma_j=[z^j]C(z)^2$, so $c_n/a_{n-1}\to1$ with first correction
$2\log n/n$ (Theorem~\ref{gdy:ren:theorem} and \eqref{gdy:ren:first}).
Part~V proves the same expansion independently
(Theorem~\ref{bnc:thm:companion}) and adds
$c_n\sim C_*(W(n)/n)\mathcal N_n$ with the constant of the Bell
normalization, which is not yet independently reviewed.

\begin{question}[Other reversion fixed points and positivity-preserving maps]
Which variants of
$M=1+zM^pB(zM^q)$ preserve normalized Stieltjes moment sequences, and
which OEIS reversion sequences have fixed points of such maps?
\end{question}
The factorization in Section~\ref{gml:sec:operators} proves the case
$(p,q)=(2,1)$. It is not a proof for arbitrary $p,q$. Positive block
operators and noncrossing transforms offer testable sufficient mechanisms;
negative Hankel minors provide exact obstructions in unsuccessful cases.

\begin{question}[A modular formalization in ProveIt]
Can the proof be formalized with reusable lemmas for formal transforms,
positive kernels, compact moment limits, and iteration rigidity?
\end{question}
A feasible first stage is the formal coefficient stabilization and the
bivariate determinant identities, followed by the finite-remainder formula
and the convex-slope lemma. The operator-to-measure limit is a separate
analytic component. The scalar rigidity lemma is comparatively isolated
and could be formalized independently. No code included here is presented
as a completed Lean formalization.

\section{Conclusion}
A088714 and A088713 are not merely two rapidly growing coefficient lists.
They are the determinate moment sequences of a coupled pair of positive
measures. This representation proves every strict Hankel inequality,
provides monotone algebraic bounds for the associated transforms, and
makes their divergent ordinary generating functions meaningful on the
negative axis through explicit Stieltjes and Borel--Laplace formulas.

The nonlinear inverse relation $U^{-1}(t)=t(1+U(t))$ forces complementary
golden-ratio exponents, including their leading constants. Part~I's
Bell-scale coefficient bound, together with the new convexity, determines
consecutive ratios and the far-tail logarithmic law. The resulting picture
is rigorous at both endpoints while leaving the finer coefficient
normalization, measure regularity, and second-order behavior clearly open.
[Batch 86, 4 October 2026.] Measure regularity and the second-order behavior at the
golden-ratio endpoint are settled in Part~IV, and the finer normalization
in Part~V, whose proof has not yet been independently reviewed.

\clearpage
\part{Local estimates for the finer Bell comparison}\label{ble:part}

\section{Source, scope and notation of Part~III}\label{ble:sec:source}

\paragraph{Summary.}
Part~II proves positive-measure realizations and consecutive-ratio
equivalents for A088714, but does not prove Part~I's finer Bell
normalization (Conjecture~\ref{conj:finer}). Part~III reconciles the two
arguments and isolates the extra precision needed. A uniform small-degree
coefficient expansion is proved without assuming any smoothness of the
ratios (Lemma~\ref{ble:lem:coef}); it is Part~I's formal near-endpoint
expansion \eqref{eq:localcoeff}, now a theorem. Combined with the
log-convexity of Part~II, it yields an unconditional additive lower
correction tending to $3/2$ (Theorem~\ref{ble:thm:lower}). The
conjectured correction tends instead to $2$; the missing half is
generated by curvature of ratios across a logarithmic window. An exact
Poisson-weighted endpoint identity reduces the conjecture to two
quantitative estimates, averaged local curvature and the mass outside that
endpoint (Theorem~\ref{ble:thm:criterion}), or to the single scalar
estimate $\mathcal E_n=O(W(n)^q/n^2)$ of \eqref{ble:eq:defect}. A
sufficient tilted-variance estimate is stated explicitly
(Proposition~\ref{ble:prop:variance}), and every subsequent implication is
proved. Exact-integer computations test both the proposed normalization
and the individual missing estimates. The full ratio expansion and the
existence or value of the normalization constant remain unproved:
\textbf{Conjecture~\ref{conj:finer} is still open after Part~III.}

\paragraph{Provenance.}
Part~III is manuscript~01 of batch~76 of the repository's incoming-reports
intake: \emph{The missing local estimate in the finer Bell comparison. An
endpoint reduction, a rigorous one-sided correction, and numerical stress
tests for OEIS A088714}, ``Research continuation prepared for Vladimir
Reshetnikov'', dated October~2, 2026 (UTC) (a 372-line source and a
10-page PDF). Its author line names no person and no model. It arrived as
\nolinkurl{A088714_Bell_Local_Estimates.zip} in commit
\texttt{6914ccca6} and was placed in commit \texttt{2a04b60f2}. The
manuscript pins the repository at commit
\texttt{82afb955918e5af9ccd2a7740424b2a66976f339} (October~1, 2026), a
descendant of the commit \texttt{0a5908ec0} that wrote Part~II. The source
of this report is the same at the pin and at the placement commit, so the
manuscript read Parts~I and~II as printed here, together with Part~II's
source audit \nolinkurl{02-golden-moments-SOURCE_AUDIT.md}; it also
consulted library copies of the two delivered articles. Its code and data
are shipped with the prefix \texttt{03-local-estimates-}. Not shipped, and
surviving in the archive of the arrival commit: its own \LaTeX\ source,
README and PDF; its copy of the repository's root \texttt{LICENSE}; its
copies of Part~I's \nolinkurl{code/coefficients.py},
\nolinkurl{code/compute_gmp.cpp} and \nolinkurl{data/diagnostics.csv} (the last
delivered as \nolinkurl{data/prior_diagnostics.csv}), which equal the shipped
files byte for byte; and its 2.49\,MB table
\nolinkurl{data/coefficients_snapshot.txt} of $a_0,\ldots,a_{1600}$, which
is regenerable (see the README) and agrees with
\nolinkurl{data/02-golden-moments-coefficients.csv} on the overlap
$n\le401$.

\paragraph{What is not reprinted.}
The manuscript's Section~2 re-derives Proposition~\ref{prop:conditional}
without saying that Part~I proves it: it assumes the same ratio estimate
\eqref{eq:neededratio}, uses the same normalizer \eqref{eq:normalizer}
and the same quotient expansion \eqref{eq:normratio}, obtained from the
same derivative identity, and reaches the same summability conclusion.
That argument is replaced by a pointer in Section~\ref{ble:sec:why}, and
the manuscript's displays of the defining equation and of the normalizer
are replaced by \eqref{eq:def} and \eqref{eq:normalizer}. The inherited
results \eqref{ble:eq:growth}--\eqref{ble:eq:ratio}, which the manuscript
itself calls ``not new assertions'', are kept as a summary, with pointers
to the theorems of Parts~I and~II that prove them. Everything else in the
manuscript is printed: all other sections, proofs and remarks, its status
table, and its list of next investigations.

\paragraph{Editorial changes.}
Section~$k$ of the manuscript is Section~$k+25$ here, and every printed
label carries the prefix \texttt{ble:}. Three symbols were renamed, to
match Parts~I and~II or to avoid a clash: the manuscript's normalizer $N_n$
is Part~I's $\mathcal N_n$ (the same function), its Bell number $B_n$ is
Part~I's $\mathsf B_n$ in the text (the shipped table fragments still
print $B_n$), and the excess degree called $e$ in the proof of
Lemma~\ref{ble:lem:coef} is $\eta$, since $e$ is also Euler's number.
No normalization changed. One sentence of
Section~\ref{ble:sec:curvature} read ``This proves \eqref{ble:eq:avg}''
although the deduction uses the hypothesis \eqref{ble:eq:local}; it now
reads ``Under \eqref{ble:eq:local}, this proves \eqref{ble:eq:avg}.''
The manuscript defined a theorem environment ``Unproved estimate'' that
it never used; it is not carried over. File names in
Section~\ref{ble:sec:numerics} are the shipped ones. In the bibliography,
the manuscript's entry for the repository is replaced by internal
references (its caveat is kept below), its OEIS entry is merged with
Part~I's, and its two remaining entries are added
\cite{moserwyman,grunwaldserafin}. The manuscript's statement that it
makes no repository modifications described the delivery. At placement,
Part~I gained dated notes at \eqref{eq:localcoeff} and after
Proposition~\ref{prop:conditional}, Part~II gained one after its question
on a full asymptotic equivalent (Section~\ref{gml:sec:research}), and
Table~\ref{gml:tab:notation} gained Part~III's letters. Nothing else in
the mathematics was changed.

\paragraph{Status.}
Part~III, like Parts~I and~II, is AI-assisted and unrefereed, and it is
not formalized: no Lean or Rocq development checks any statement of this
report, and placement in this collection confers no formal status. The
manuscript treats Parts~I and~II as ``written research reports, not
assumed to be peer reviewed or formally checked''. It makes no priority
claim; ``new'' in it means new relative to Parts~I and~II. Its numerical
tests are finite (all $n\le1600$) and are not used in any proof.

\paragraph{Related repository material.}
The Bell asymptotic behind $\mathcal N_n$ is \eqref{eq:bellsaddle}
\cite{dlmf-bell,moserwyman}. An all-orders form of the Bell saddle
expansion, with $W(n)$ as the saddle $we^w=n$, is Theorem
\texttt{q2:thm:bell}, with Remark \texttt{q2:rem:bell-scale}, of the
transseries volume in
\begin{quote}\small\ttfamily
Analysis/Transseries/docs/series-and-transseries/\\
Transseries\_And\_Inversion/
\end{quote}
Part~III uses $W(n)$ only as that saddle. The tilt $\mu_{n-1}$ of
Section~\ref{ble:sec:curvature} is Part~II's size-biased law $\sigma_{n-1}$
for $\sigma=\mu$ (proof of Lemma~\ref{gml:lem:tails}), and identity
\eqref{ble:eq:var} is the relative-variance identity of that proof with
the ratio index shifted by one.

\paragraph{Notation.}
Part~III uses the letters of Part~I's Section~\ref{sec:finer},
$w=W(n)$, $t=n/r_n$ and $d=w/(1+w)$, but writes the backward ratio
$a_n/a_{n-1}$ as $r_n$. Every symbol of Part~III is local to Part~III;
the lower block of Table~\ref{gml:tab:notation} lists each letter it
shares with Part~I or Part~II, with all meanings. The tempting false
readings: $R(n)$ below is the candidate right side of
\eqref{eq:ratiocandidate}, \emph{not} a ratio, while Part~I's ratio $R_n$
is Part~III's $r_n$; and Part~II's $r_n=a_{n+1}/a_n$ is a forward ratio,
Part~III's $r_{n+1}$.

\section{Sources, notation, and current proof status}\label{ble:sec:status}
Let $A(z)=\sum_{n\ge0}a_nz^n$ be the formal solution of \eqref{eq:def}
(Part~I writes $x$ for the variable), so that $a_0=1$, $a_1=1$, $a_2=3$,
$a_3=13$. Throughout Part~III,
\[
 r_n=\frac{a_n}{a_{n-1}},\quad w=W(n),\quad
 R(n)=\frac n w+2+\frac{w}{2(1+w)^2},\quad d=\frac{w}{1+w}.
\]
Part~II's source audit, dated October~1, says explicitly that the earlier
finer Bell normalization is not established. The manuscript inspected
Part~I (the September~20 Bell-scale article), Part~II (the October~1
golden-moment work) and that audit at the pin recorded in
Section~\ref{ble:sec:source}.

The usable conclusions of that work are
\begin{align}
 \log a_n&=n\log n-n\log\log n-n+O(n\log\log n/\log n),\label{ble:eq:growth}\\
 a_n&=\int_0^\infty x^n\,d\mu(x),\label{ble:eq:moment}\\
 r_n&\sim n/\log n\sim n/W(n),\qquad r_{n+1}\ge r_n.\label{ble:eq:ratio}
\end{align}
They are Theorem~\ref{thm:main}, Theorem~\ref{gml:thm:mainmoment}, and
Theorem~\ref{gml:thm:maintail} with the monotonicity of the ratios given by
the strict log-convexity of Theorem~\ref{gml:thm:infiniteLC}.
The probability measure $\mu$ has all positive exponential moments finite.
Its normalized Stieltjes transform satisfies
\[
 F(s)=\int\frac{d\mu(x)}{1+sx},\qquad
 F(s)=1-sF(s)^2F(sF(s)),
\]
and, with $\alpha=(3-\sqrt5)/2$, $F(s)\sim s^{-\alpha}$ as $s\to\infty$
(Theorem~\ref{gml:thm:mainendpoint}). These are inherited results, not new
assertions of Part~III.

\begin{center}\small
\begin{tabular}{>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{.28\linewidth}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{.31\linewidth}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{.31\linewidth}}\toprule
Item & What is proved & What remains needed\\\midrule
New consecutive ratios & $r_n\sim n/\log n$ & Additive corrections at constant scale\\
Quantitative ratio bound & Relative error $O(\sqrt{\log\log n/\log n})$ & Additive error $O(w^p/n)$ after $R(n)$\\
Moment representation & Relative tilted variance tends to zero & Relative variance $d/n$ with error $O(w^q/n^2)$ is a sufficient input\\
Golden transform law & Mass near zero and its leading constant & Quantitative information about high-moment tilts in the far tail\\
This continuation & Uniform coefficient expansion; one-sided correction; conditional reduction & Curvature and complementary-endpoint estimates\\\bottomrule
\end{tabular}\end{center}

\section{Why the new ratio theorem does not settle normalization}\label{ble:sec:why}
The convex-slope proof (Lemma~\ref{gml:lem:slopes} and the corollary after
Proposition~\ref{gml:prop:companion}) uses secants over intervals of length
$\delta n$. The global error in \eqref{ble:eq:growth} contributes
$O(\log\log n/(\delta\log n))$ to such a secant, and curvature of the
comparison function contributes $O(\delta)$. Optimizing gives a relative
ratio error of order $\sqrt{\log\log n/\log n}$. This is much too large even
to distinguish a constant correction in $r_n$.

In particular, a relative $o(1)$ error in one ratio can accumulate over
infinitely many ratios. Neither strict Hankel positivity nor strict
iterated log-convexity provides the particular numerical curvature
coefficient required below.

The $s\to\infty$ transform law concerns $x\downarrow0$. High moments tilt
toward $x$ of order $n/W(n)\to\infty$. Improving the former endpoint law
does not by itself improve the latter regime. The exact transform equation
still contains the whole sequence, but using it for high moments requires
a new uniform asymptotic argument; differentiating a cumulative-mass law
or treating a divergent ordinary series as convergent would not supply
one.

Part~I's normalizer $\mathcal N_n$ of \eqref{eq:normalizer} satisfies
$\mathsf B_n\exp(w^2+3w)\sim\mathcal N_n$ by the classical Bell asymptotics
\eqref{eq:bellsaddle} \cite{moserwyman}. The exact summability threshold
is Proposition~\ref{prop:conditional}: if
\[
 r_n=R(n)+O(w^p/n)
\]
for some fixed $p$, which is \eqref{eq:neededratio}, then
$a_n/\mathcal N_n$ tends to a finite positive constant. (The manuscript
re-derives this: $\mathcal N_n/\mathcal N_{n-1}=R(n)+O(w^3/n)$ is
\eqref{eq:normratio}, and the successive differences of
$\log(a_n/\mathcal N_n)$ are $O(w^{\max(p,3)+1}/n^2)$, as in the proof of
Proposition~\ref{prop:conditional}.) This argument proves convergence, but
does not determine the constant from its asymptotic local input.

More generally, absolute summability of
$|r_n-\mathcal N_n/\mathcal N_{n-1}|/r_n$ suffices. The fixed-power
condition \eqref{eq:neededratio} is convenient rather than necessary.

\section{A coefficient estimate that can be proved now}\label{ble:sec:coef}
Direct coefficient extraction in \eqref{eq:def} gives the exact endpoint
recurrence
\begin{equation}\label{ble:eq:rec}
 a_n=\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}a_k[z^{n-1-k}]A(z)^{k+2}
 =\sum_{m=0}^{n-1}a_{n-1-m}b_{n,m},
 \quad b_{n,m}=[z^m]A(z)^{n+1-m}.
\end{equation}
(The first sum is Part~I's \eqref{eq:directrec}; the second puts
$m=n-1-k$.) All terms are nonnegative. Only coefficients of finite degree
are used.

\begin{lemma}[Uniform logarithmic-window coefficient expansion]\label{ble:lem:coef}
For every fixed $C>0$, uniformly for integers $0\le m\le C\log n$,
\begin{equation}\label{ble:eq:b}
 b_{n,m}=\frac{n^m}{m!}\left(1+\frac{3m(m-1)}{2n}
                    +O\!\left(\frac{(1+m)^6}{n^2}\right)\right).
\end{equation}
No local ratio smoothness hypothesis is used.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Equation \eqref{ble:eq:growth} implies $(a_j/j!)^{1/j}\to0$.
Consequently there is a finite $K\ge1$ with $a_j\le K^{j-1}j!$ for every
$j\ge2$.
Put $M=n+1-m$. The coefficient in question is obtained by choosing
nonconstant terms from $M$ factors. The contribution using only $m$
singletons is $(M)_m/m!$. Its normalized value is
\[
 \frac{(M)_m}{n^m}
 =\prod_{h=0}^{m-1}\left(1+\frac{1-m-h}{n}\right)
 =1-\frac{3m(m-1)}{2n}+O((1+m)^4/n^2).
\]
The contribution with one degree-two term and $m-2$ singletons is
$3(M)_{m-1}/(m-2)!$, whose normalized value is
\[
 \frac{3m(m-1)}n\{1+O((1+m)^2/n)\}.
\]
The sum of these two contributions supplies the displayed first
correction.

For completeness, all higher patterns admit a uniform bound. Let $l_j$
count the chosen degree-$j$ terms for $j\ge2$. Put
$\eta=\sum_{j\ge2}(j-1)l_j$ and $s=\sum_{j\ge2}jl_j$. The number of chosen
nonconstant factors is $m-\eta$; there are $m-s$ singletons. After
normalization by $n^m/m!$, the contribution is at most
\[
 n^{-\eta}\frac{m!}{(m-s)!}\prod_{j\ge2}\frac{a_j^{l_j}}{l_j!}
 \le \left(\frac{K m^3}{n}\right)^{\eta}.
\]
Here $s\le2\eta$, $j!\le m^{j-1}$ for $2\le j\le m$, and
$(M)_{m-\eta}\le n^{m-\eta}$. For fixed $\eta$, the possible lists $(l_j)$
are partitions of $\eta$, at most $2^\eta$ in number. Summing over
$\eta\ge2$ bounds their total by a geometric series of order $m^6/n^2$,
since $m=O(\log n)$. The cases $m=0,1$ are exact. This proves
\eqref{ble:eq:b}.
\end{proof}

For two small degrees, exact certificates are
\[
 C_{n,2}=1+\frac3n-\frac4{n^2},\qquad
 C_{n,3}=1+\frac9n+\frac{14}{n^2}-\frac{72}{n^3},
\]
where $C_{n,m}=m!\,b_{n,m}/n^m$ as in \eqref{ble:eq:C} below. They agree
with the coefficient $3m(m-1)/2$ above and are independently checked in
the supplied code (\nolinkurl{code/03-local-estimates-diagnose.py} asserts
both as exact integer identities at $n=10$, $100$, $1000$ and $10000$).

This disposes of one previously formal ingredient: the coefficient
expansion itself, Part~I's \eqref{eq:localcoeff}, is valid uniformly in
the relevant growing window. The unresolved input is the coefficient ratio
multiplying it, and the contribution outside the window.

\section{An unconditional one-sided additive correction}\label{ble:sec:lower}
\begin{theorem}[Lower correction from moment positivity]\label{ble:thm:lower}
Assuming the inherited results \eqref{ble:eq:growth}--\eqref{ble:eq:ratio},
\begin{equation}\label{ble:eq:lower}
 r_n\ge \frac n{W(n)}+\frac32+\frac{1}{2(1+W(n))}
             -O\!\left(\frac{W(n)^4}{n}\right).
\end{equation}
In particular,
\[
 \liminf_{n\to\infty}\left(r_n-\frac n{W(n)}\right)\ge\frac32.
\]
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Let $L=\lceil16w\rceil$ and $t=n/r_n$. For sufficiently large $n$,
$L<n-1$. Monotonicity of the ratios gives
\[
 \frac{a_{n-1-m}}{a_n}=\prod_{j=0}^{m}r_{n-j}^{-1}\ge r_n^{-m-1}.
\]
The $k=0$ term of \eqref{ble:eq:rec}, disjoint from $0\le m\le L$, is
$[z^{n-1}]A^2\ge2a_{n-1}$. Thus Lemma~\ref{ble:lem:coef} implies
\[
 1\ge \frac1{r_n}\sum_{m=0}^L\frac{t^m}{m!}
 \left(1+\frac{3m(m-1)}{2n}-O(w^6/n^2)\right)+\frac2{r_n}.
\]
By \eqref{ble:eq:ratio}, $t/w\to1$. The standard Chernoff estimate,
obtained by applying Markov's inequality to $e^{uZ}$ for
$Z\sim\operatorname{Poisson}(t)$, shows that the probability of $Z>L$ and
its fixed polynomial moments are $O(n^{-10})$. Indeed, for large $n$,
$t\le2w$ and $L\ge16w$; the exponent at $L/t\ge8$ is at most
$-(16\log8-16+2)w$, much smaller than $-10\log n$ asymptotically.
First the preceding lower sum shows $e^t/r_n$ is bounded. Completing the
Poisson sums is consequently justified with an error smaller than
$O(w^6/n^2)$, giving
\begin{equation}\label{ble:eq:lowerH}
 1\ge H_n(r_n)-O(w^6/n^2),\qquad
 H_n(r)=\frac{e^{n/r}}r\left(1+\frac{3n}{2r^2}\right)+\frac2r.
\end{equation}
This function is strictly decreasing for $r>0$. Put
\[
 s_n=\frac n w+c(w),\qquad
 c(w)=\frac{2+3w/2}{1+w}=\frac32+\frac1{2(1+w)}.
\]
Since $e^w=n/w$, expansion yields
\[
 H_n(s_n)=1+O(w^4/n^2),\qquad
 -H_n'(r)\asymp w^2/n
\]
uniformly in a fixed bounded neighborhood of $s_n$.
For a sufficiently large constant $C_1$, evaluation at $s_n-C_1w^4/n$
contradicts \eqref{ble:eq:lowerH} if $r_n$ lies below that point.
Monotonicity handles points further below. This gives the stated error
$O(w^4/n)$.
\end{proof}

The gap between this lower correction and the conjectured correction is
\[
 \left(2+\frac{w}{2(1+w)^2}\right)
 -\left(\frac32+\frac1{2(1+w)}\right)
 =\frac12-\frac1{2(1+w)^2}.
\]
It tends to $1/2$. This is precisely the extra contribution supplied by
the local curvature calculation in the next section. The lower bound does
not prove the conjectured upper bound.

\section{An exact Poisson reduction and a sharp sufficient lemma}\label{ble:sec:criterion}
Fix $L=\lceil16w\rceil<n-1$, and define
\begin{align}
 p_t(m)&=e^{-t}\frac{t^m}{m!},\quad t=\frac n{r_n},\label{ble:eq:p}\\
 D_{n,m}&=r_n^{m+1}\frac{a_{n-1-m}}{a_n}
          =\prod_{j=0}^{m}\frac{r_n}{r_{n-j}},\label{ble:eq:D}\\
 C_{n,m}&=\frac{m!b_{n,m}}{n^m},\label{ble:eq:C}\\
 J_n&=\frac1{a_n}\sum_{m=L+1}^{n-1}a_{n-1-m}b_{n,m}.\label{ble:eq:J}
\end{align}
Then \eqref{ble:eq:rec} is exactly
\begin{equation}\label{ble:eq:exact}
 1=\frac{e^t}{r_n}\sum_{m=0}^{L}p_t(m)D_{n,m}C_{n,m}+J_n.
\end{equation}
There is no infinite-series substitution into $A$ here.

\begin{theorem}[Averaged endpoint criterion]\label{ble:thm:criterion}
Suppose that for some fixed $q\ge0$ the actual sequence satisfies
\begin{align}
 \sum_{m=0}^{L}p_t(m)D_{n,m}C_{n,m}
 &=1+\frac{(3+d)t^2+2dt}{2n}+O(w^q/n^2),\label{ble:eq:avg}\\
 J_n&=\frac2{r_n}+O(w^q/n^2).\label{ble:eq:comp}
\end{align}
Then \eqref{eq:neededratio} holds for some fixed $p$, and the finer Bell
normalization converges to a finite strictly positive constant.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
By \eqref{ble:eq:ratio}, $t\asymp w$ and $r_n\asymp n/w$. Equations
\eqref{ble:eq:exact}--\eqref{ble:eq:comp} first show
$e^t/r_n=1+O(w^2/n)+O(w^q/n^2)$. Hence
\[
 t-\log r_n=O(w^2/n)+O(w^q/n^2).
\]
Since $r\mapsto n/r-\log r$ has derivative $-(t+1)/r\asymp-w^2/n$ near its
zero $n/w$, the mean value theorem implies $r_n=n/w+O(1)$ (fixed powers of
$w$ divided by $n$ tend to zero).
Write $r_n=n/w+\delta$, $\delta=O(1)$. Then
\[
 \frac{e^t}{r_n}=1-\frac{\delta w(1+w)}n+O(w^4/n^2),\qquad
 t=w+O(w^2/n).
\]
Insert these in \eqref{ble:eq:exact}. With $Q=\max(q,6)$,
\[
 \delta w(1+w)=\frac{(3+d)w^2+2dw}{2}+2w+O(w^Q/n).
\]
Therefore
\[
 \delta=\frac{2+(3+d)w/2+d}{1+w}+O(w^Q/n)
       =2+\frac{w}{2(1+w)^2}+O(w^Q/n).
\]
This is \eqref{eq:neededratio}; Proposition~\ref{prop:conditional}
completes the proof.
\end{proof}

The two hypotheses are the missing estimates, not proved conclusions. The
averaged criterion is weaker than a uniform local expansion: rare values
of $m$ may behave differently if their weighted contribution is
controlled. It avoids asking for a density of $\mu$ or a complete tail
transseries. (In Part~I's terms, under \eqref{ble:eq:avg} and
\eqref{ble:eq:comp} the exact identity \eqref{ble:eq:exact} is the formal
balance \eqref{eq:formalbalance} with an error $O(w^q/n^2)$, and the proof
above is Part~I's formal derivation of \eqref{eq:ratiocandidate} made
rigorous under those hypotheses.)

[Batch 86, 4 October 2026.] Part~V proves \eqref{ble:eq:comp} with $q=2$,
unconditionally (Theorem~\ref{bnc:thm:endpoint}):
$J_n=2/r_n+5/(r_nr_{n-1})+O(w^3/n^3)$. Estimate \eqref{ble:eq:avg} remains
unproved; Part~V reaches the convergence of the finer normalization
without it (Theorem~\ref{bnc:thm:bell}), in a proof that has not yet been
independently reviewed.

There is also a logically minimal scalar defect. Define
\begin{equation}\label{ble:eq:defect}
 \mathcal E_n=
 \frac{e^t}{r_n}\left\{\sum_{m\le L}p_t(m)D_{n,m}C_{n,m}
 -1-\frac{(3+d)t^2+2dt}{2n}\right\}
 +J_n-\frac2{r_n}.
\end{equation}
The exact identity says
\[
 1=\frac{e^t}{r_n}\left(1+\frac{(3+d)t^2+2dt}{2n}\right)
       +\frac2{r_n}+\mathcal E_n.
\]
Thus the single estimate $\mathcal E_n=O(w^q/n^2)$ suffices. It allows
cancellation between the two errors, whereas
\eqref{ble:eq:avg}--\eqref{ble:eq:comp} controls them separately. By
substituting \eqref{eq:neededratio} into this identity one sees that some
such fixed-power bound on $\mathcal E_n$ is also necessary for
\eqref{eq:neededratio}. This scalar reformulation is sharp in precision,
but the separated estimates explain what must be analyzed.

\section{Local curvature: the moment representation's precise missing input}\label{ble:sec:curvature}
The following uniform condition is sufficient for the averaged estimate:
\begin{equation}\label{ble:eq:local}
 \log r_{n-j}-\log r_n=-\frac{dj}{n}
                +O\!\left(\frac{w^q}{n^2}\right),
 \qquad 0\le j\le L,
\end{equation}
for a fixed $q$; polynomial factors in $j\le16w+1$ can be absorbed into a
larger $q$.
Summing this relation in \eqref{ble:eq:D} gives, uniformly,
\begin{equation}\label{ble:eq:localD}
 D_{n,m}=1+\frac{d\,m(m+1)}{2n}+O(w^{q'}/n^2).
\end{equation}
Multiply by Lemma~\ref{ble:lem:coef} and use
\[
 \E[Z(Z-1)]=t^2,\qquad \E[Z(Z+1)]=t^2+2t,
 \quad Z\sim\operatorname{Poisson}(t),
\]
with the same Chernoff truncation as before. Under \eqref{ble:eq:local},
this proves \eqref{ble:eq:avg}. It is now clear exactly where the
coefficient $d$ comes from. Monotonicity alone gives $D_{n,m}\ge1$ and
loses this contribution.

The moment formulation is especially transparent. Define the probability
tilt
\[
 d\mu_{n-1}(x)=\frac{x^{n-1}}{a_{n-1}}d\mu(x).
\]
Then the following identities are exact:
\begin{equation}\label{ble:eq:var}
 \E_{\mu_{n-1}}X=r_n,\qquad
 \frac{\Var_{\mu_{n-1}}X}{r_n^2}
 =\frac{r_{n+1}}{r_n}-1.
\end{equation}
\begin{proposition}[Tilted variance sufficient for local curvature]\label{ble:prop:variance}
A sufficient input for \eqref{ble:eq:local} is the all-large-index
estimate
\begin{equation}\label{ble:eq:varlemma}
 \frac{\Var_{\mu_{i-1}}X}{r_i^2}
 =\frac{W(i)}{i(1+W(i))}+O\!\left(\frac{W(i)^q}{i^2}\right).
\end{equation}
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Take the logarithm of $1+$ the right side of \eqref{ble:eq:var}:
\[
 \log r_{i+1}-\log r_i
 =\frac{W(i)}{i(1+W(i))}+O(W(i)^{\max(q,0)}/i^2).
\]
On $[n-L,n]$, the derivative of $W(x)/(x(1+W(x)))$ is $O(n^{-2})$.
Telescoping over $j\le L=O(w)$ therefore gives \eqref{ble:eq:local}, after
enlarging the fixed exponent to absorb $j$ and $j^2$.
\end{proof}

The golden-moment ratio law gives only $\Var_{\mu_{i-1}}X/r_i^2\to0$.
Estimate \eqref{ble:eq:varlemma} requires its leading value at scale
$1/i$, and an error at scale $\operatorname{polylog}(i)/i^2$. Even a
first-order equivalent at scale $1/i$ is insufficient for the displayed
summable-error target unless its error is quantified much more sharply.
The variance condition is a sufficient route, not asserted to be
necessary: the averaged condition \eqref{ble:eq:avg} could hold without
it.

A conventional saddle analysis would aim at mean near $n/w$, variance near
$n/[w(1+w)]$, and higher central moments with uniform errors. But Part~II
has not proved a density or a local tail law that permits such an
analysis. Assuming such a density would move the gap rather than close
it.

\section{The complementary endpoint is an independent missing estimate}\label{ble:sec:complement}
The $k=0$ term in \eqref{ble:eq:rec} contains $2a_{n-1}$. For
\eqref{ble:eq:comp} one must bound everything else outside $m\le L$ by
\begin{equation}\label{ble:eq:remainderlemma}
 \sum_{m=L+1}^{n-1}a_{n-1-m}b_{n,m}-2a_{n-1}
       =O\!\left(a_n\frac{w^q}{n^2}\right).
\end{equation}
Its left side is nonnegative for sufficiently large $n$, since $k=0$ lies
in the complementary range. Thus this is a positive upper-bound problem,
and it cannot rely on cancellation inside that range.

For orientation, degree concentration in the first two outer terms
supplies a lower-order contribution. The $k=0$ term has
$2a_{n-1}+2a_1a_{n-2}+\cdots$; the $k=1$ term has $3a_1a_{n-2}+\cdots$.
Hence, for large enough $n$ that both terms lie outside the endpoint
window,
\[
 J_n-\frac2{r_n}\ge\frac{5a_{n-2}}{a_n}
 =\frac5{r_nr_{n-1}}\sim\frac{5w^2}{n^2}.
\]
This explains why it would be wrong to discard the complementary error as
smaller than $w^2/n^2$. A bound of that order is compatible with the
desired normalization.

Similarly, the main Poisson endpoint has second-order contributions from
two degree-two choices, degree-three choices, and the square of the local
curvature. Its natural next error is of polynomial-logarithmic order
divided by $n^2$, which is precisely sufficient after solving for the
ratio. The current proof establishes the small-degree coefficient part,
but does not establish a uniform upper bound for all configurations
containing a large inner coefficient. The inherited $r_n\sim n/\log n$
controls fixed neighboring ratios but does not automatically justify
summing a growing number of such configurations.

[Batch 86, 4 October 2026.] Part~V supplies that uniform upper bound by decomposing
according to the position of the unique large inner index:
Theorem~\ref{bnc:thm:endpoint} gives \eqref{ble:eq:remainderlemma} with
$q=2$, and in fact the complementary sum to every fixed order, beginning
$2a_{n-1}+5a_{n-2}+24a_{n-3}$.

\section{Numerical stress tests and their limits}\label{ble:sec:numerics}
The accompanying computation generates $a_0,\ldots,a_{1600}$ as exact
integers by the inherited triangular recurrence of
Section~\ref{sec:numerics} (the delivered run used the C adapter
\nolinkurl{code/03-local-estimates-compute_runtime.c}; its output table is
not shipped). The script \nolinkurl{code/03-local-estimates-diagnose.py}
checks a prefix independently with Python, compares against Part~I's
stored high-precision ratios (\nolinkurl{data/diagnostics.csv}), and checks
\eqref{eq:def} through degree 35 using independent finite polynomial
multiplication. Exact Bell numbers are generated with the Bell triangle.
Decimal logarithms, exponentials and a Newton solution of $we^w=n$ provide
the diagnostic arithmetic. Runs at 80 and 120 decimal digits agree: all
reported diagnostics pass the comparison thresholds, with maximum observed
absolute difference below $2\times10^{-75}$
(\nolinkurl{data/03-local-estimates-precision_check.json}). This checks
numerical stability. No limiting claim is derived from those checks.

For each index we record
\[
 \Delta_n=r_n-R(n),\quad
 D_n^{B}=\log(a_n/\mathsf B_n)-w^2-3w,\quad
 D_n^{N}=\log(a_n/\mathcal N_n).
\]
The first normalization uses exact Bell numbers; the second uses the
smooth leading model. Confusing them at finite $n$ would contaminate a
remainder fit. ($D_n^B$ is Part~I's $D_n^{\mathrm{num}}$ of
\eqref{eq:Dnum}; Table~\ref{ble:tab:results}, whose $B_n$ is
$\mathsf B_n$, agrees with Table~\ref{tab:diagnostics} at $n\le1200$, and
its column $r_n-R(n)$ is Part~I's $R_n-n/W(n)$ minus
$2+w/(2(1+w)^2)$.)
\input{data/03-local-estimates-results_table.tex}
{\lasttabletarget\label{ble:tab:results}}

The observed scaled ratio residual $n\Delta_n/w^2$ is a diagnostic for a
candidate $O(w^2/n)$ remainder, considerably stronger than an unscaled
plot. Its bounded appearance is evidence only. A finite range cannot
exclude a later term such as $1/w^k$, which would be much larger than any
fixed $w^p/n$ and could destroy normalization convergence.

For selected indices, the endpoint sum is computed exactly before Decimal
conversion. Coefficients of $A(z)^M$ are generated by the independent
recurrence
\[
 b_0=1,\qquad s b_s=\sum_{j=1}^{s}\big((M+1)j-s\big)a_jb_{s-j},
\]
which follows from $A(A^M)'=MA'A^M$. Every division is checked to have
zero integer remainder. This directly measures $J_n$ as $1-$ the endpoint
sum, without assuming endpoint dominance.
\input{data/03-local-estimates-endpoint_table.tex}
{\lasttabletarget\label{ble:tab:endpoint}}

The complementary residual's approach to $5$ in its natural scaling
(Table~\ref{ble:tab:endpoint}) agrees with the two explicit $a_{n-2}$
contributions above. This does not prove a matching upper bound. We also
test the curvature average, the local-window log-ratio error, and the
complete scalar balance defect. Their full values and the cutoffs are in
the CSV and JSON outputs:
\begin{itemize}[nosep]
\item all indices: \nolinkurl{data/03-local-estimates-diagnostics.csv};
\item the selected indices:
 \nolinkurl{data/03-local-estimates-endpoint_diagnostics.json};
\item the window sweep: \nolinkurl{data/03-local-estimates-window_sweep.json}.
\end{itemize}
Changing the window coefficient from 16 to 8, 12 and 24 checks sensitivity to truncation.
Windows with the larger constants can require substantially larger $n$ to
be asymptotic; the diagnostic does not claim uniformity beyond the stated
finite values.

The normalization seems compatible with a finite positive limit and with
the summable ratio remainder. No certified value of that limit is
supplied. In particular, proximity to $e^{-3}$ in a finite range would not
prove an exact constant. Initial coefficients affect the normalization
constant, so a local all-orders expansion alone would not automatically
identify it.

\section{What has been resolved and the next precise task}\label{ble:sec:resolved}
The new proof and the old sufficient criterion are consistent. The new
ratio law is an essential enabling result because it gives monotone
ratios and a legitimate moment tilt; its error estimate remains far from
the normalization precision.

This continuation proves the uniform coefficient expansion and the
additive lower correction \eqref{ble:eq:lower}. It also proves every
implication from the precise missing estimates to the desired ratio and
Bell normalization. It does not prove those missing estimates for A088714,
and therefore does not claim a proof of the full finer comparison.

The sharpest single remaining lemma in this formulation is
\[
 \boxed{\mathcal E_n=O(W(n)^q/n^2)\quad\text{for some fixed }q.}
\]
The more informative two-part route is \eqref{ble:eq:avg} plus
\eqref{ble:eq:remainderlemma}. From the moment representation,
\eqref{ble:eq:varlemma} supplies a concrete sufficient curvature estimate.
An attack through the endpoint recurrence should prove the complementary
positive bound and bootstrap the local curvature without presupposing the
target ratio expansion. Using the target expansion to justify the window
estimate would be circular.

Useful next investigations are: (i) a positive combinatorial upper bound
for configurations with a large inner coefficient; (ii) a stability
theorem for the endpoint balance that controls discrete curvature from the
exact recurrence; (iii) quantitative high-moment tilt estimates from the
coupled resolvents, without assuming absolute continuity; and (iv) a
higher-order calculation retaining the $5a_{n-2}$ term, followed by a
proved error bound. Only after those steps should one attempt a certified
interval for the normalization constant or all-orders transseries
inversion.

[Batch 86, 4 October 2026.] Items (i) and (iv) are carried out in Part~V
(Theorem~\ref{bnc:thm:endpoint}), which then proves the finer comparison
without the curvature estimate (Section~\ref{bnc:sec:bellconvergence});
that proof has not yet been independently reviewed. A certified interval
for the constant remains open (Research question~\ref{bnc:q:constant}).

\clearpage
\part{Densities, the correction profile, and the companion sequence}\label{gdy:part}

\section{Source, scope and notation of Part~IV}\label{gdy:sec:source}

\paragraph{Summary.}
Let $A(z)=1+zA(z)^2A(zA(z))$ and $C(z)=(1-zA(z))^{-1}$, and let $\mu,\nu$
be the probability measures of Part~II with moments $a_n$ and $c_n$.
Part~IV answers three of Part~II's Research questions
(Section~\ref{gml:sec:research}).
\begin{itemize}[nosep]
\item \emph{Question 23.1, regularity and support.} Both measures have
strictly positive real-analytic densities throughout $(0,\infty)$ and
support exactly $[0,\infty)$; with $\beta=(\sqrt5-1)/2$ and
$\alpha=1-\beta$,
$p(x)\sim\pi^{-1}\sin(\pi\alpha)\,x^{-\beta}$ and
$q(x)\sim\pi^{-1}\sin(\pi\beta)\,x^{-\alpha}$ as $x\downarrow0$, with
every fixed derivative (Theorem~\ref{gdy:reg:thm:main},
Corollary~\ref{gdy:reg:cor:derivatives}). A coupled inverse relation
rules out every hypothetical positive density zero; a complex inversion
argument gives the endpoint equivalents, without differentiating the
distribution-function laws.
\item \emph{Question 23.3, second-order golden-ratio asymptotics.} There
is a nonzero real-analytic function $P$ with $P(t+1)=-P(t)$ such that
$\log(s^\alpha F(s))=P(\log_\varphi\log s)/\log s+O((\log s)^{-3})$,
$\varphi=(1+\sqrt5)/2$, together with an explicit expansion to all
inverse-logarithmic orders that converges for large $\log s$, with an
exponentially smaller, separately bounded discrepancy
(Theorem~\ref{gdy:dyn:thm:allorders}). An interval certificate proves
$P\not\equiv0$, so the correction oscillates and the $O(1/\log s)$ rate
is optimal (Corollary~\ref{gdy:dyn:cor:sharp}).
\item \emph{Question 23.6, the companion sequence.} For every fixed $K$,
$c_n=\sum_{j=0}^{K}\gamma_ja_{n-1-j}+O_K(a_{n-K-2})$ with
$\gamma_j=[z^j]C(z)^2$; in particular $c_n/a_{n-1}\to1$ with first
relative correction $2\log n/n$ (Theorem~\ref{gdy:ren:theorem}). The
theorem is proved for every log-convex sequence with
$a_n/a_{n-1}\sim n/\log n$, by a weighted-composition argument that
assumes no fine expansion of the ratios.
\end{itemize}
Part~IV does not bear on Part~I's finer Bell normalization
(Conjecture~\ref{conj:finer}); Part~V, from another manuscript of the same
batch, proves it.

\paragraph{Provenance.}
Part~IV is the part on A088714 and A088713 of manuscript~01 of batch~86
of the repository's incoming-reports intake: \emph{Three advances on OEIS
conjectures and asymptotics. Bridgeless toroidal maps, golden-ratio
moment laws, and unitary-divisor partitions}, ``Prepared with ChatGPT,
building on Vladimir Reshetnikov's ProveIt research repository'', dated
4~October 2026 (UTC) (a 2{,}894-line source and a 40-page PDF). It
arrived as \nolinkurl{oeis_advances.zip} in commit \texttt{ae9baa422} and
was placed in commit \texttt{0f084afa9}. The manuscript pins the
repository at commit
\texttt{eaf08931cbfd0132dd36ab82a7743ccb02506dc7} (4~October 2026,
00:59:12 UTC); the source of this report is the same at the pin and at

here. Its Part~II (Sections~3--6) is printed here as Sections~36--39,
and the corresponding passages of its Sections~1, 8 and~9 as this

Every label carries the prefix \texttt{gdy:}; the manuscript's labels

Part~IV, like Parts~I--III, is AI-assisted and unrefereed, and it is not
