\section{Inherited inputs and the new target}\label{bnc:sec:bell-inputs}

In Part~V \(a_n\) denotes A088714 as defined by \eqref{eq:def}.
We use Part~I's \(R_n=a_n/a_{n-1}\) for its consecutive ratio (Part~III
writes \(r_n\)). The manuscript chose \(R_n\) to avoid the symbol \(r_k\)
of its other part, on A321941, which is not printed here.
All series involving \(A\) are formal unless a finite polynomial
or an explicitly convergent expression is specified.

\subsection{The precise earlier theorems}

We use three results of Parts~I and~II, in the form in which Part~III
states them as \eqref{ble:eq:growth}--\eqref{ble:eq:ratio} (with \(r_n\)
there for \(R_n\) here): the growth law \eqref{ble:eq:growth}, which is
Theorem~\ref{thm:main}; the moment representation \eqref{ble:eq:moment}
by a probability measure \(\mu\), Theorem~\ref{gml:thm:mainmoment}; and
\(R_n\sim n/\log n\sim n/W(n)\) with \(R_{n+1}\ge R_n\),
\eqref{ble:eq:ratio}, which is Theorem~\ref{gml:thm:maintail} together
with the log-convexity of Theorem~\ref{gml:thm:infiniteLC}.

The logical origin of these inputs is useful.  The first uses an
exact triangular recurrence and upper comparison with shifted
Poisson moments.  The second constructs moment-preserving positive
operators, then passes to the coefficientwise attracting fixed
point of the functional equation.  Cauchy--Schwarz applied to
\eqref{ble:eq:moment} gives log-convexity, hence monotonicity
of \(R_n\).  Finally, secant bounds for the convex sequence
\(\log a_n\), combined with \eqref{ble:eq:growth}, prove the
ratio equivalent; an \(n\)-th-root equivalent by itself would
not justify that step.

For clarity, the secant argument is short.  Fix \(0<\varepsilon<1\)
and bracket \(\log R_n\) between slopes over the intervals
\([(1-\varepsilon)n,n]\) and \([n,(1+\varepsilon)n]\), with
integer endpoints.  Insert \eqref{ble:eq:growth}, first let
\(n\to\infty\), and then let \(\varepsilon\downarrow0\).
This gives
\(\log R_n=\log n-\log\log n+o(1)\).
Two consequences used repeatedly below are
\[
 a_n^{1/n}\sim\frac n{e\log n},\qquad
 \frac{a_n^{1/n}}{R_n}\longrightarrow e^{-1}.
\]

We do not need the stronger total-positivity or endpoint-transform
theorems of Part~II.  The new results below follow from
\eqref{ble:eq:growth}--\eqref{ble:eq:ratio} and the
coefficient estimate recalled next.

\subsection{The logarithmic-window coefficient estimate}

Extracting coefficients from \eqref{eq:def} gives Part~III's exact
recurrence \eqref{ble:eq:rec},
\[
 a_n=\sum_{m=0}^{n-1}a_{n-1-m}b_{n,m},\qquad
 b_{n,m}=[z^m]A(z)^{n+1-m}.
\]
Every summand is nonnegative. Lemma~\ref{ble:lem:coef} of Part~III gives,
for each fixed \(C>0\) and uniformly over integers \(0\le m\le C\log n\),
the expansion \eqref{ble:eq:b},
\[
 b_{n,m}=\frac{n^m}{m!}
 \left(1+\frac{3m(m-1)}{2n}
       +O_C\!\left(\frac{(1+m)^6}{n^2}\right)\right).
\]
(The manuscript restates that lemma with its proof ``to make the
subsequent uniform estimates directly checkable''; the proof is Part~III's,
step for step, and is not reprinted.)

\subsection{The normalization to be proved}

For real \(x>0\), put \(w_x=W(x)\) and define
\begin{equation}\label{bnc:eq:normalizer}
 \mathcal N(x)=
 \frac{\exp\!\left(x(w_x-1+1/w_x)-1+w_x^2+3w_x\right)}
      {\sqrt{1+w_x}},\qquad
 \mathcal N_n=\mathcal N(n),\quad \mathcal N_0=1.
\end{equation}
The principal real Lambert function has
\[
 W'(x)=\frac{W(x)}{x(1+W(x))}
 \quad\cite{dlmf-w}.
\]
At integers \(n\ge1\) this is Part~I's normalizer \eqref{eq:normalizer}.
The classical Bell saddle equivalent is Part~I's \eqref{eq:bellsaddle}
\cite{moserwyman,dlmf-bell}.
Thus \(\mathcal N_n\sim\mathsf B_ne^{w^2+3w}\).

The main theorem of Part~V, proved in
Section~\ref{bnc:sec:bellconvergence}, is
\begin{equation}\label{bnc:eq:bellmainoverview}
 \boxed{a_n=C_*\mathcal N_n
         \left(1+O\!\left(\frac{W(n)^5}{n}\right)\right),
         \qquad 0<C_*<\infty.}
\end{equation}
The quantitative error in \eqref{bnc:eq:bellmainoverview} is stated
relative to the explicit \(\mathcal N_n\).
Using only the Bell equivalent \eqref{eq:bellsaddle} then gives
\(a_n\sim C_*\mathsf B_ne^{w^2+3w}\).

The proof has three stages.  First, a decomposition by the position
of the unique large inner index resolves the complementary endpoint.
Second, a smooth comparison sequence gives positive upper and lower
barriers for \(a_n/\mathcal N_n\).  Third, a summation identity for
slowly varying Poisson-tail kernels proves convergence of the
bounded normalized sequence and gives its rate.  The final step
requires only \(R_n=n/W(n)+O(1)\), which we prove separately.


\section{The complementary endpoint to every fixed order}\label{bnc:sec:endpoint}

This section proves the complementary-endpoint estimate left open in
Part~III, the hypothesis \eqref{ble:eq:comp} of
Theorem~\ref{ble:thm:criterion}, and extends it to every fixed order.

\subsection{Statement}

Let \(A(z)=\sum_{n\ge0}a_nz^n\) be the unique formal solution of
\eqref{eq:def}.
Thus \(a_0=a_1=1\), \(a_2=3\), \(a_3=13\).
The exact coefficient recurrence is \eqref{ble:eq:rec}, whose first form
\(a_n=\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}a_k[z^{n-1-k}]A(z)^{k+2}\) is Part~I's
\eqref{eq:directrec}; there \(b_{n,m}=[z^m]A(z)^{n+1-m}\).
For a fixed constant \(C>0\), put
\[
 w=W(n),\quad L=\lceil Cw\rceil,\quad
 T_n(L)=\sum_{m=L+1}^{n-1}a_{n-1-m}b_{n,m}.
\]
All assertions below concern sufficiently large \(n\), so that
\(L<n/2-2\).

Define a formal series \(D(z)=\sum_{h\ge0}\delta_hz^h\) by
\[
 D(z)=A(z)\bigl(2A(zA(z))+zA(z)A'(zA(z))\bigr).
\]
Its coefficients are nonnegative.  More explicitly,
\[
 \delta_h=\sum_{k=0}^{h}(k+2)a_k[z^{h-k}]A(z)^{k+1}.
\]
The initial values are
\[
 \delta_0=2,\quad \delta_1=5,\quad \delta_2=24,\quad \delta_3=148,\quad
 \delta_4=1052,\quad \delta_5=8226.
\]

\begin{theorem}[Complementary endpoint to any fixed order]\label{bnc:thm:endpoint}
Fix a nonnegative integer \(P\) and a constant \(C\) satisfying
\[
 C\log 2>P+5.
\]
Then
\[
 \boxed{
 T_n(\lceil CW(n)\rceil)
   =\sum_{h=0}^{P}\delta_h a_{n-1-h}
      +O_P(a_{n-P-2}) .}
\]
The implied constant may also depend on \(C\).
In particular, for Part~III's cutoff \(L=\lceil16W(n)\rceil\),
\[
 \boxed{
 \frac{T_n(L)}{a_n}
   =\frac2{R_n}+\frac5{R_nR_{n-1}}
       +O\!\left(\frac{W(n)^3}{n^3}\right).}
\]
Consequently the previously unproved estimate
\[
 \frac{T_n(L)}{a_n}
       =\frac2{R_n}
           +O\!\left(\frac{W(n)^2}{n^2}\right)
\]
holds unconditionally under the inherited theorems.
\end{theorem}

In Part~III's notation \(T_n(L)=a_nJ_n\), with \(J_n\) as in
\eqref{ble:eq:J}. The theorem supplies the complementary hypothesis
\eqref{ble:eq:comp} of Part~III with \(q=2\).
It also proves the numerical prediction from Part~III's endpoint table
(Table~\ref{ble:tab:endpoint}):
\[
 \frac{n^2}{W(n)^2}
   \left(\frac{T_n(L)}{a_n}-\frac2{R_n}\right)\longrightarrow5.
\]
This is a precise improvement on Part~III's one-sided lower bound
\(J_n-2/r_n\ge5/(r_nr_{n-1})\) (Section~\ref{ble:sec:complement}).
For \(C=16\) the general theorem applies to every integer \(P\le6\).
For arbitrary fixed \(P\), one convenient sufficient choice is
\(C=2P+16\).

\subsection{A truncated-series estimate}

Throughout the proof set \(N=\lfloor n/2\rfloor\) and
\(A_N(z)=\sum_{j=0}^{N}a_jz^j\).
No convergent interpretation is given to the divergent formal series \(A\).

\begin{lemma}[Uniform finite-polynomial bound]\label{bnc:lem:finitepoly}
For \(z_n=4/R_n\),
\[
 A_N(z_n)=1+z_n+O(z_n^2),\qquad
 A_N(z_n)^{n+3}=\exp((4+o(1))W(n)).
\]
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Log-convexity says that the graph of \(\log a_j\) lies below
the chord joining its values at any two indices.
For \(2\le j\le N\), put
\[
 q_n=\left(\frac{a_N}{a_2}\right)^{1/(N-2)}.
\]
Then \(a_j\le a_2q_n^{j-2}\).
The inherited root and ratio equivalents give
\[
 \frac{q_n}{R_n}\longrightarrow\frac1{2e}.
\]
Indeed \(q_n/a_N^{1/N}\to1\), because
\(\log a_N=O(N\log N)\), and
\(a_N^{1/N}\sim N/(e\log N)\), while
\(R_n\sim n/\log n\).
It follows that \(z_nq_n\to2/e<1\).  Hence
\[
 0\le A_N(z_n)-1-z_n
 \le \frac{a_2z_n^2}{1-z_nq_n}=O(z_n^2).
\]
Also \(n/R_n\sim W(n)\), and \(n/R_n^2\to0\).
Taking logarithms proves the second assertion.
\end{proof}

The strict inequality \(2/e<1\) is why the evaluation point \(4/R_n\)
works.  This estimate uses only a finite polynomial of degree
\(\lfloor n/2\rfloor\).

\subsection{The region with a large outer index}

Write \(H=n-N-2\), so that \(H\sim n/2\).
The terms whose outer index is \(k>N\) correspond exactly to
\(0\le m\le H\).
For \(m\le H\le N\), nonnegativity gives
\[
 b_{n,m}\le A_N(z_n)^{n+1}z_n^{-m}.
\]
Monotone ratios give
\[
 \frac{a_{n-1-m}}{a_n}
 =\prod_{j=n-m}^{n}R_j^{-1}
 \le R_{N+2}^{-(m+1)}.
\]
Put \(Q_n=R_n/R_{N+2}\to2\).
Therefore, for any \(L<H\),
\[
 \frac1{a_n}
 \sum_{m=L+1}^{H}a_{n-1-m}b_{n,m}
 \le
 \frac{Q_n}{R_n}A_N(z_n)^{n+1}
       \sum_{m>L}(Q_n/4)^m.
\]
The geometric series is valid for all sufficiently large \(n\).
Since \(R_n\sim e^{w}\), for \(L=\lceil Cw\rceil\) this gives
\begin{equation}\label{bnc:eq:bell-1}
\frac1{a_n}
 \sum_{m=L+1}^{H}a_{n-1-m}b_{n,m}
 \le \exp\bigl((3-C\log2+o(1))w\bigr).
\end{equation}
For a fixed \(P\),
\[
 \frac{a_{n-P-2}}{a_n}
   =\prod_{j=0}^{P+1}R_{n-j}^{-1}
   \sim e^{-(P+2)w}.
\]
The strict inequality \(C\log2>P+5\) makes the right side of
\eqref{bnc:eq:bell-1} \(o(a_{n-P-2}/a_n)\).
Thus this entire region is negligible at the claimed precision.

\subsection{The region with no large inner index}

Now consider \(k\le N\).
Every such term has \(m=n-1-k\ge n-1-N>L\), so this entire
region belongs to the complementary sum \(T_n(L)\).
Expand the coefficient of \(A(z)^{k+2}\) as a sum over ordered tuples
\((j_1,\ldots,j_{k+2})\) with
\[
 k+j_1+\cdots+j_{k+2}=n-1.
\]
Let \(E_n\) be the contribution for which every \(j_i\le N\).
Using the finite-polynomial bound and positivity,
\[
 \begin{split}
 E_n
 &=\sum_{k=0}^{N}a_k[z^{n-1-k}]A_N(z)^{k+2}\\
 &\le z_n^{-(n-1)}
       \sum_{k=0}^{N}a_kz_n^kA_N(z_n)^{k+2}\\
 &\le z_n^{-(n-1)}A_N(z_n)^{N+3}.
 \end{split}
\]
Here the sum is at most \(A_N(z_n)^{N+2}\cdot A_N(z_n)\), the last factor being \(\sum_{k\le N}a_kz_n^k\).
Since \(a_n^{1/n}/R_n\to e^{-1}\),
\[
 \log(E_n/a_n)
 \le n(1-\log4+o(1)).
\]
In particular \(E_n=O(a_ne^{-cn})\) for some fixed \(c>0\).
This is \(o(a_{n-P-2})\) for every fixed \(P\).

\subsection{The unique large inner index}

Every remaining configuration has an inner index \(j_i>N\).
There can be at most one, since \(j_1+\cdots+j_{k+2}\le n-1<2(N+1)\).
Write that unique index as \(n-1-h\).
Then \(0\le h\le H\), the outer index satisfies \(0\le k\le h\),
and the remaining inner indices sum to \(h-k\).
All these remaining indices are automatically at most \(N\).
There are \(k+2\) choices for the location of the large inner index.
Consequently this region is exactly
\begin{equation}\label{bnc:eq:bell-2}
\sum_{h=0}^{H}a_{n-1-h}
       \sum_{k=0}^{h}(k+2)a_k[z^{h-k}]A(z)^{k+1}
 =\sum_{h=0}^{H}\delta_h a_{n-1-h}.
\end{equation}

The coefficientwise inequality \(A^{k+1}\le A^{k+2}\) and
the defining recurrence imply
\begin{equation}\label{bnc:eq:bell-3}
0\le \delta_h\le(h+2)a_{h+1}.
\end{equation}
We need the following elementary convolution consequence of the
inherited ratio equivalent.

\begin{lemma}[A weighted endpoint tail]\label{bnc:lem:endpointtail}
For every fixed integer \(p\ge0\),
\begin{equation}\label{bnc:eq:bell-4}
\sum_{h=p}^{H}(h+2)a_{h+1}a_{n-1-h}
       =O_p(a_{n-1-p}).
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Let \(V_h=a_{h+1}a_{n-1-h}\).
Then
\[
 \frac{V_{h+1}}{V_h}
      =\frac{R_{h+2}}{R_{n-1-h}}\le1
 \qquad(0\le h<H).
\]
For \(K=\lfloor n/3\rfloor\), uniformly over \(0\le h\le K\),
\[
 \frac{V_{h+1}}{V_h}
 \le \frac{R_{K+2}}{R_{n-1-K}}\longrightarrow\frac12.
\]
Thus the ratio is at most \(3/4\) on that range for large \(n\).
Starting at the fixed index \(p\), the part up to \(K\) is bounded by
\[
 V_p\sum_{u\ge0}(p+u+2)(3/4)^u=O_p(V_p).
\]
Monotonicity bounds the rest of the sum by
\[
O(n^2)(3/4)^{K-p}V_p=O_p(V_p).
\]
Finally \(V_p=a_{p+1}a_{n-1-p}\), and \(a_{p+1}\) is fixed.
\end{proof}

Apply \eqref{bnc:eq:bell-4} with \(p=P+1\), use \eqref{bnc:eq:bell-3}, and remove the first \(P+1\)
terms from \eqref{bnc:eq:bell-2}.  The remaining sum is \(O_P(a_{n-P-2})\).
Combining this with the two negligible regions proves the theorem.

\subsection{An identity for the endpoint coefficients}

The coefficients \(\delta_h\) need not be computed by a nested composition.
Set \(B(z)=zA(z)\).  The original equation is
\[
 B(z)=z+B(z)B(B(z)).
\]
Differentiate, divide by \(B'(z)\), and use
\(B'(z)=1+O(z)\):
\[
 1-\frac1{B'(z)}
     =B(B(z))+B(z)B'(B(z)).
\]
The right side equals \(zD(z)\), so
\[
 \boxed{D(z)=\frac1z\left(1-\frac1{(zA(z))'}\right).}
\]
This identity is entirely formal.
It yields the triangular exact-integer recurrence
\[
 \delta_h=(h+2)a_{h+1}
        -\sum_{i=1}^{h}(i+1)a_i \delta_{h-i}.
\]
The subtraction in this computational formula does not obscure
positivity, which is already proved by the composition formula.

\subsection{Consequence for the remaining Bell-normalization problem}

At the existing cutoff \(L=\lceil16W(n)\rceil\), the complete exact
recurrence now has the rigorously controlled form
\[
 a_n=
 \sum_{m=0}^{L}b_{n,m}a_{n-1-m}
       +2a_{n-1}+5a_{n-2}+O(a_{n-3}).
\]
Part~III's uniform coefficient theorem (Lemma~\ref{ble:lem:coef}) supplies
\[
 b_{n,m}=\frac{n^m}{m!}
 \left(1+\frac{3m(m-1)}{2n}
       +O\!\left(\frac{(1+m)^6}{n^2}\right)\right)
 \qquad(0\le m\le L).
\]
Thus the large-inner-coefficient obstruction listed in Part~III
(Section~\ref{ble:sec:complement}) is removed.  Its curvature estimate
\eqref{ble:eq:local} remains unproved:
\[
 \log R_{n-j}-\log R_n
 =-\frac{W(n)}{1+W(n)}\,\frac jn
     +O\!\left(\frac{W(n)^q}{n^2}\right),
 \qquad 0\le j\le L.
\]
The next sections establish convergence for the normalized
positive recurrence, whose lags are concentrated near
\(1+\Poisson (W(n))\).  This approach proves multiplicative Bell normalization without first deriving every pointwise curvature estimate.  The following sections develop that
alternative: positive barriers first give bounded-factor normalization,
and a summation argument then proves convergence.


\section{Positive barriers for the Bell normalizer}\label{bnc:sec:bellbarriers}

The complementary-endpoint estimate also yields a stronger asymptotic
conclusion without the still-open pointwise curvature lemma.
It pins the conjectured normalization to within two fixed positive
multiplicative constants.

Let \(\mathcal N(x)\), \(\mathcal N_n=\mathcal N(n)\) for \(n\ge1\), and
\(\mathcal N_0=1\) be as in \eqref{bnc:eq:normalizer}.
The classical Bell-number saddle asymptotic gives
\[
 \mathcal N_n\sim \mathsf B_n\exp(W(n)^2+3W(n)).
\]
Here \(\mathsf B_n\) denotes the Bell number (OEIS A000110).
The same explicit normalizer is Part~I's \eqref{eq:normalizer}, and the
Bell asymptotic is Part~I's \eqref{eq:bellsaddle}.

\begin{theorem}[Bounded-factor Bell normalization]\label{bnc:thm:bellbounds}
There exist constants \(0<c<C<\infty\) such that
\[
 c\mathcal N_n\le a_n\le C\mathcal N_n
 \qquad(n\ge0).
\]
Equivalently,
\[
 \boxed{
 a_n=\Theta\!\left(\mathsf B_n\exp(W(n)^2+3W(n))\right).}
\]
In logarithmic form, with \(w=W(n)\),
\[
 \boxed{
 \log a_n
 =n(w-1+1/w)-1+w^2+3w-\tfrac12\log(1+w)+O(1).}
\]
\end{theorem}

This does not assert that \(a_n/\mathcal N_n\) converges.
It proves that the ratio is bounded and bounded away from zero.
The theorem improves Part~I's
\(n\)-th-root asymptotic to a bounded additive error in the logarithm.
Its proof uses a Taylor expansion of the explicit comparison function
\(\mathcal N\), and makes no smoothness assumption about the actual
sequence \(a_n\).

\subsection{Local ratios of the explicit comparison function}

Write
\[
 f(x)=\log\mathcal N(x),\qquad
 s_n=\frac{\mathcal N_n}{\mathcal N_{n-1}},\qquad
 w=W(n),\qquad d=\frac{w}{1+w}.
\]
Using \(W'(x)=W(x)/(x(1+W(x)))\), direct differentiation gives
\[
 f'(x)=W(x)+\frac{1}{x}
 \frac{W(x)}{1+W(x)}
 \left(2W(x)+3-\frac{1}{2(1+W(x))}\right).
\]
Consequently
\begin{equation}\label{bnc:eq:bell-5}
f''(x)=\frac{W(x)}{x(1+W(x))}
          +O\!\left(\frac{W(x)}{x^2}\right),
 \qquad
 f'''(x)=O(x^{-2}).
\end{equation}
These bounds hold uniformly when \(x\) varies over
\([n-O(W(n)),n]\).

Taylor's theorem applied to \(f(n)-f(n-1)\) gives
\[
 \log s_n
 =w+\frac{d}{n}
    \left(2w+\frac52-\frac1{2(1+w)}\right)
       +O(w/n^2).
\]
Thus, putting
\[
 \vartheta(w)=2+\frac{w}{2(1+w)^2},
\]
so that \(n/w+\vartheta(w)\) is Part~III's candidate \(R(n)\),
we obtain
\begin{equation}\label{bnc:eq:bell-6}
s_n=\frac n w+\vartheta(w)+O(w/n).
\end{equation}
The identity behind the constant correction is
\[
 \frac{d}{w}
    \left(2w+\frac52-\frac1{2(1+w)}\right)
       =\vartheta(w).
\]

Let \(L=\lceil16w\rceil\).
For \(0\le j\le L\), apply \eqref{bnc:eq:bell-5} to
\(g(x)=f(x)-f(x-1)\).
Since
\[
 g'(x)=\frac{W(x)}{x(1+W(x))}
            +O(W(x)/x^2),
\]
integration over \([n-j,n]\) gives, uniformly,
\begin{equation}\label{bnc:eq:bell-7}
\log s_{n-j}-\log s_n
 =-\frac{dj}{n}+O(w^2/n^2).
\end{equation}
There is no claim that \eqref{bnc:eq:bell-7} holds for \(R_n=a_n/a_{n-1}\).

For \(0\le m\le L\), define the model correction
\[
 E_{n,m}
 =s_n^{m+1}\frac{\mathcal N_{n-1-m}}{\mathcal N_n}
 =\prod_{j=0}^{m}\frac{s_n}{s_{n-j}}.
\]
Summing \eqref{bnc:eq:bell-7}, then exponentiating, gives
\begin{equation}\label{bnc:eq:bell-8}
E_{n,m}
 =1+\frac{d\,m(m+1)}{2n}+O(w^4/n^2),
\end{equation}
uniformly on the same window.

\subsection{The comparison recurrence has summable row error}

We now use the already-proved coefficient expansion of
Part~III (Lemma~\ref{ble:lem:coef}):
\begin{equation}\label{bnc:eq:bell-9}
b_{n,m}
 =\frac{n^m}{m!}
   \left(1+\frac{3m(m-1)}{2n}
          +O\!\left(\frac{(1+m)^6}{n^2}\right)\right),
 \qquad0\le m\le L.
\end{equation}
It is important that \eqref{bnc:eq:bell-9} is a theorem about coefficients of a finite
degree; it assumes no neighboring-ratio asymptotic beyond the
inherited root-growth bound.

Define the model row sum
\[
 S_n=
 \sum_{m=0}^{L}b_{n,m}
          \frac{\mathcal N_{n-1-m}}{\mathcal N_n}
       +2\frac{\mathcal N_{n-1}}{\mathcal N_n}.
\]

\begin{lemma}[Summable model row error]\label{bnc:lem:rowsum}
For \(L=\lceil16W(n)\rceil\),
\begin{equation}\label{bnc:eq:bell-10}
S_n=1+O\!\left(\frac{W(n)^6}{n^2}\right).
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Put \(t=n/s_n\).
Multiplying \eqref{bnc:eq:bell-8} and \eqref{bnc:eq:bell-9} yields
\[
 \begin{split}
 &\sum_{m=0}^{L}b_{n,m}
          \frac{\mathcal N_{n-1-m}}{\mathcal N_n}\\
 &\quad=
 \frac1{s_n}\sum_{m=0}^{L}\frac{t^m}{m!}
 \left(1+\frac{3m(m-1)+d\,m(m+1)}{2n}
        +O(w^6/n^2)\right).
 \end{split}
\]
Here \(t\sim w\) and \(e^t/s_n\to1\).
A Poisson Chernoff bound shows that the discarded tails after
\(L=\lceil16w\rceil\), including all fixed polynomial moments,
are \(O(n^{-10})\).
Therefore
\begin{equation}\label{bnc:eq:bell-11}
S_n=\frac{e^t}{s_n}
 \left(1+\frac{(3+d)t^2+2dt}{2n}
         +O(w^6/n^2)\right)+\frac2{s_n}.
\end{equation}

Equation \eqref{bnc:eq:bell-6} implies
\[
 t=w-\frac{\vartheta(w)w^2}{n}+O(w^3/n^2),
 \qquad
 \frac{e^t}{s_n}
 =1-\frac{\vartheta(w)w(1+w)}n+O(w^4/n^2).
\]
Also \(2/s_n=2w/n+O(w^2/n^2)\).
The coefficient of \(1/n\) in \eqref{bnc:eq:bell-11} is
\[
 -\vartheta(w)w(1+w)
   +\frac{(3+d)w^2+2dw}{2}+2w=0.
\]
The equality is an exact rational identity in \(w\).
This proves \eqref{bnc:eq:bell-10}.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Positive barriers}

From the complementary-endpoint theorem and positivity,
there is a fixed \(K<\infty\) such that, for every sufficiently
large \(n\),
\begin{equation}\label{bnc:eq:bell-12}
T_n(L)=2a_{n-1}+\mathcal R_n,\qquad
 0\le \mathcal R_n\le K a_{n-2}.
\end{equation}
The lower inequality can also be read directly from the \(k=0\)
term of the defining recurrence.
For the upper inequality, the theorem gives
\(\mathcal R_n=5a_{n-2}+O(a_{n-3})\), and
\(a_{n-3}/a_{n-2}\to0\).

Let \(u_n=a_n/\mathcal N_n>0\), and set
\[
 \mathcal Q_n(u)=
 \sum_{m=0}^{L}b_{n,m}
       \frac{\mathcal N_{n-1-m}}{\mathcal N_n}\,u_{n-1-m}
       +2\frac{\mathcal N_{n-1}}{\mathcal N_n}\,u_{n-1}.
\]
Dividing the exact coefficient recurrence by \(\mathcal N_n\)
and using \eqref{bnc:eq:bell-12} gives
\begin{equation}\label{bnc:eq:bell-13}
\mathcal Q_n(u)\le u_n
 \le \mathcal Q_n(u)
      +K\frac{\mathcal N_{n-2}}{\mathcal N_n}\,u_{n-2}.
\end{equation}
All coefficients are nonnegative, and all referenced indices are
strictly smaller than \(n\).
By \eqref{bnc:eq:bell-6},
\(\mathcal N_{n-2}/\mathcal N_n=O(w^2/n^2)\).
Together with \eqref{bnc:eq:bell-10}, this supplies constants \(K_1,n_0\) such that,
for \(n\ge n_0\), both the lower and upper row sums in \eqref{bnc:eq:bell-13}
lie between \(1-\varepsilon_n\) and \(1+\varepsilon_n\), where
\[
 0\le\varepsilon_n=K_1W(n)^6/n^2\le\tfrac12,
 \qquad \sum_{n\ge n_0}\varepsilon_n<\infty.
\]

Set
\[
 m_n=\min_{0\le j\le n}u_j,\qquad
 M_n=\max_{0\le j\le n}u_j.
\]
Since every coefficient in \eqref{bnc:eq:bell-13} is nonnegative,
\[
 m_n\ge m_{n-1}(1-\varepsilon_n),
 \qquad
 M_n\le M_{n-1}(1+\varepsilon_n)
 \qquad(n\ge n_0).
\]
Iteration gives
\[
 m_n\ge m_{n_0-1}
       \prod_{j=n_0}^{n}(1-\varepsilon_j),\qquad
 M_n\le M_{n_0-1}
       \prod_{j=n_0}^{n}(1+\varepsilon_j).
\]
The first infinite product is positive and the second finite, because
\(\sum\varepsilon_j<\infty\) and \(0\le\varepsilon_j\le1/2\).
The finite initial segment has positive minimum and finite maximum.
This proves the bounded-factor normalization theorem.

\subsection{From bounded factors to a convergent normalization}

The new conclusions are:
\[
 \frac{T_n(\lceil16W(n)\rceil)}{a_n}
 =\frac2{R_n}+\frac5{R_nR_{n-1}}+O(W(n)^3/n^3),
 \qquad
 0<\inf_n\frac{a_n}{\mathcal N_n}
       \le\sup_n\frac{a_n}{\mathcal N_n}<\infty.
\]
Thus both divergence to infinity and decay to zero of the conjectured
normalized sequence have been excluded.

The positive-barrier argument by itself does not prove existence of a limit
\[
 \lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{a_n}{\mathcal N_n}.
\]
The next section proves that limit using a summation lemma for the
slowly varying Poisson averages.  The sharper individual-ratio expansion
and identification of the normalization constant remain further
research questions.


\section{Convergence of the finer Bell normalization}\label{bnc:sec:bellconvergence}

This section completes the normalization conjecture by summing a
slowly varying averaging equation.  No pointwise second-difference
estimate for the actual ratios is assumed.

\begin{theorem}[Finer Bell normalization]\label{bnc:thm:bell}
There is a finite strictly positive constant \(C_*\) such that
\[
 \boxed{
 a_n=C_*\mathcal N_n
      \left(1+O\!\left(\frac{W(n)^5}{n}\right)\right).}
\]
Consequently
\[
 \boxed{
 a_n\sim C_*\mathsf B_n\exp(W(n)^2+3W(n)).}
\]
Here \(\mathcal N_n\) is Part~I's normalizer \eqref{eq:normalizer}.
The proof establishes the existence of \(C_*\), without identifying its
numerical value or an exact expression in familiar constants.
It does not establish the stronger conjectured pointwise expansion
\(R_n=n/W(n)+2+W(n)/(2(1+W(n))^2)
+O(W(n)^p/n)\).
\end{theorem}

\subsection{Constant-scale control of the actual ratios}

\begin{lemma}[An unconditional bounded additive ratio error]\label{bnc:lem:actualratio}
With \(R_n=a_n/a_{n-1}\) and \(w=W(n)\),
\begin{equation}\label{bnc:eq:bell-14}
R_n=\frac n w+O(1).
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
The inherited ratio equivalent gives \(R_n\sim n/w\).
Put \(L_n=\lceil16W(n)\rceil\) and \(t=n/R_n\).
The coefficient estimate on this window implies
\begin{equation}\label{bnc:eq:bell-15}
\sum_{m=0}^{L_n} b_{n,m}R_n^{-m-1}
  =\frac{e^t}{R_n}\left(1+O(w^2/n)\right).
\end{equation}
The uniform estimate before Poisson summation is
\(b_{n,m}=n^m/m!\,(1+O(w^2/n))\);
the discarded Poisson tail is \(O(n^{-10})\).
This reasoning is valid whenever the trial denominator is
asymptotic to \(n/w\).

Monotonicity of the actual ratios gives
\(a_{n-1-m}/a_n\ge R_n^{-m-1}\).
The exact recurrence and positivity therefore imply
\[
 1\ge \frac{e^{n/R_n}}{R_n}(1+O(w^2/n)).
\]
The parenthesized factor is positive for large \(n\).
Taking logarithms yields
\[
 \frac n{R_n}-\log R_n\le O(w^2/n).
\]
The function \(F_n(r)=n/r-\log r\) is strictly decreasing, has
zero \(n/W(n)\), and has derivative of magnitude comparable to
\(w^2/n\) on any interval whose points are asymptotic to \(n/w\).
The mean value theorem yields \(R_n\ge n/w-O(1)\).

For the upper bound, set
\[
 v=n+\lceil20W(n)\rceil.
\]
For sufficiently large \(n\), \(v-L_v\ge n\), since
\(W(v)-W(n)=O(W(n)/n)\).
Thus every denominator \(R_{v-j}\) in
\(a_{v-1-m}/a_v=\prod_{j=0}^{m}R_{v-j}^{-1}\),
for \(0\le m\le L_v\), is at least \(R_n\).
The complementary-endpoint theorem gives \(T_v(L_v)/a_v=O(w/n)\).
Apply \eqref{bnc:eq:bell-15} at \(v\), with trial denominator \(R_n\sim v/W(v)\).
The exact recurrence now implies
\[
 1\le \frac{e^{v/R_n}}{R_n}
           (1+O(w^2/n))+O(w/n).
\]
It follows that \(F_v(R_n)\ge-O(w^2/n)\).
The same mean-value argument gives
\(R_n\le v/W(v)+O(1)\).
Since
\[
 \frac{d}{dx}\frac{x}{W(x)}=\frac1{1+W(x)}
\]
and \(v-n=O(w)\), we have \(v/W(v)=n/W(n)+O(1)\).
This proves \eqref{bnc:eq:bell-14}.
\end{proof}

Put
\[
 u_n=\frac{a_n}{\mathcal N_n},\quad
 y_n=\log u_n,\quad
 \xi_n=y_n-y_{n-1}\quad(n\ge1).
\]
The bounded-factor theorem says \(y_n=O(1)\).
Combining \eqref{bnc:eq:bell-14} with the model-ratio expansion gives
\begin{equation}\label{bnc:eq:bell-16}
\xi_n=O(W(n)/n).
\end{equation}
For convenience extend \(y_n=y_0\) when \(n\le0\), and set
\(\xi_n=0\) when \(n\le0\).

\subsection{A Poisson averaging equation with summable error}

Let
\[
 p_w(m)=e^{-w}\frac{w^m}{m!}\quad(m\ge0),\qquad w=W(n).
\]
Define
\[
 \Xi_n
 =y_n-\sum_{m\ge0}p_w(m)y_{n-1-m}.
\]

\begin{lemma}[Linearized Poisson equation]\label{bnc:lem:linearpoisson}
For the actual normalized sequence,
\begin{equation}\label{bnc:eq:bell-17}
\Xi_n=O(W(n)^6/n^2).
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Write \(L=\lceil16w\rceil\),
\[
 v_{n,m}=b_{n,m}\frac{\mathcal N_{n-1-m}}{\mathcal N_n},
 \qquad s_n=\frac{\mathcal N_n}{\mathcal N_{n-1}},
\]
and
\[
 \ell_{n,m}=y_n-y_{n-1-m}.
\]
By \eqref{bnc:eq:bell-16}, uniformly for \(m\le L\),
\begin{equation}\label{bnc:eq:bell-18}
|\ell_{n,m}|
 \le K(m+1)w/n=O(w^2/n).
\end{equation}
The exact recurrence and
\(T_n(L)=2a_{n-1}+O(a_{n-2})\)
give, after division by \(a_n\),
\begin{equation}\label{bnc:eq:bell-19}
1=\sum_{m=0}^{L}v_{n,m}e^{-\ell_{n,m}}
       +\frac2{s_n}e^{-\xi_n}+O(w^2/n^2).
\end{equation}
Here the remainder is \(O(a_{n-2}/a_n)=O(w^2/n^2)\);
no unknown normalization constant occurs.

The already-proved model row sum is
\[
 \sum_{m=0}^{L}v_{n,m}+\frac2{s_n}
 =1+O(w^6/n^2).
\]
Taylor's estimate \(e^{-x}=1-x+O(x^2)\), \eqref{bnc:eq:bell-18}, and the bounded
row sum transform \eqref{bnc:eq:bell-19} into
\begin{equation}\label{bnc:eq:bell-20}
\sum_{m=0}^{L}v_{n,m}\ell_{n,m}
       +\frac2{s_n}\xi_n
 =O(w^6/n^2).
\end{equation}

Uniformly for \(m\le L\), the explicit model calculations give
\begin{equation}\label{bnc:eq:bell-21}
v_{n,m}=p_w(m)\left(1+O(w^2/n)\right).
\end{equation}
To see this directly, put \(t=n/s_n\).
Then
\[
 v_{n,m}
 =\frac{e^t}{s_n}p_t(m)
   E_{n,m}\frac{m!b_{n,m}}{n^m},
\]
where \(E_{n,m}\) is the model correction from \eqref{bnc:eq:bell-8}.
Every factor other than \(p_t(m)\) is \(1+O(w^2/n)\);
also \(t-w=O(w^2/n)\), whence
\[
 \log\frac{p_t(m)}{p_w(m)}
 =w-t+m\log(t/w)=O(w^2/n).
\]
This proves \eqref{bnc:eq:bell-21}.

Replacing \(v_{n,m}\) by \(p_w(m)\) in \eqref{bnc:eq:bell-20} costs
\(O(w^4/n^2)\) by \eqref{bnc:eq:bell-18}.
The term \(2\xi_n/s_n\) is \(O(w^2/n^2)\).
Finally, \(y_n\) is bounded, so the omitted Poisson tail after
\(L\) contributes \(O(n^{-10})\).
The result is \eqref{bnc:eq:bell-17}.
\end{proof}

\subsection{A summation lemma for slowly varying Poisson kernels}

For \(w>0\), define
\[
 q_w(j)=\mathbb P(\Poisson (w)\ge j)
 \quad(j=0,1,\ldots),
 \qquad
 k_w(j)=\frac{q_w(j)}{1+w}.
\]
Since
\[
 \sum_{j\ge0}q_w(j)=1+w,
\]
the \(k_w(j)\) form a probability distribution.
For an integer \(n\ge1\), abbreviate
\(k_n(j)=k_{W(n)}(j)\).

\begin{lemma}[Column stability and vanishing boundary]\label{bnc:lem:columns}
Define
\[
 \kappa_i=\sum_{j\ge0}k_{i+j}(j)\qquad(i\ge1).
\]
Then \(\kappa_i<\infty\), and
\begin{equation}\label{bnc:eq:bell-22}
\kappa_i=1+O(W(i)/i).
\end{equation}
If a sequence \(\xi_i\) satisfies \(\xi_i=O(W(i)/i)\), then
\begin{equation}\label{bnc:eq:bell-23}
B_N:=
 \sum_{i=1}^{N}\xi_i
       \sum_{j>N-i}k_{i+j}(j)
 =O(W(N)^2/N).
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
First consider the derivative in the Poisson parameter.
For \(j\ge1\),
\[
 \frac{\partial}{\partial w}q_w(j)=p_w(j-1),
\]
whereas \(q_w(0)=1\).
Consequently, for every \(j\ge0\),
\begin{equation}\label{bnc:eq:bell-24}
\left|\frac{\partial}{\partial w}k_w(j)\right|
       \le\frac{2}{1+w}.
\end{equation}

Fix large \(i\), write \(w=W(i)\), and set \(J=\lceil16w\rceil\).
For \(0\le j\le J\), the inequality \(W'(x)\le1/x\) gives
\[
 0\le W(i+j)-W(i)\le j/i.
\]
By \eqref{bnc:eq:bell-24},
\begin{equation}\label{bnc:eq:bell-25}
\sum_{j=0}^{J}|k_{i+j}(j)-k_i(j)|
 \le\frac{2}{i(1+w)}\sum_{j=0}^{J}j
 =O(w/i).
\end{equation}

The tails are much smaller.
For \(J<j\le i\), stochastic monotonicity of Poisson laws and
\(W(i+j)\le W(2i)\le w+\log2\) give
\[
 k_{i+j}(j)
 \le\frac{q_{w+\log2}(j)}{1+w}.
\]
For a Poisson variable \(M\) of mean \(\lambda=w+\log2\),
\[
 \sum_{j>J}q_\lambda(j)
 =\mathbb E(M-J)_+
 \le\lambda\,\mathbb P(M\ge J).
\]
The usual exponential Markov bound at \(J\sim16w\) shows that,
after division by \(1+w\), this is \(O(i^{-10})\).
The same estimate applies to \(\sum_{j>J}k_i(j)\).

For \(j>i\), \(W(i+j)\le\log(2j)\) for sufficiently large \(i\).
The Poisson Chernoff inequality gives
\[
 q_{W(i+j)}(j)
 \le\left(\frac{e\log(2j)}{j}\right)^j\le2^{-j},
\]
uniformly for such \(j\) and all sufficiently large \(i\).
Thus \(\sum_{j>i}k_{i+j}(j)=O(2^{-i})\).
The same bound for each fixed \(i\), after ignoring a finite prefix,
proves \(\kappa_i<\infty\).
Combining these tail estimates with \eqref{bnc:eq:bell-25} and
\(\sum_jk_i(j)=1\) proves \eqref{bnc:eq:bell-22}.

For \eqref{bnc:eq:bell-23}, set \(J=\lceil16W(N)\rceil\).
For indices \(N-J<i\le N\), \eqref{bnc:eq:bell-22} gives \(\kappa_i\le2\) for large \(N\).
Their contribution in absolute value is at most
\[
 2J\max_{N-J<i\le N}|\xi_i|
       =O(W(N)^2/N).
\]
For the remaining indices \(i\le N-J\), rearrange the nonnegative
upper bound using \(n=i+j>N\).
The sequence \(\xi_i\) is bounded, so it suffices to bound
\[
 \sum_{n>N}\sum_{i\le N-J}k_n(n-i).
\]
For \(N<n\le2N\), every index \(n-i\) is at least \(J\), and
\(W(n)\le W(N)+\log2\).
The same Poisson-tail argument gives
\[
 \sum_{i\le N-J}k_n(n-i)=O(N^{-10})
\]
uniformly.  Summing over these \(n\) gives \(O(N^{-9})\).
For \(n>2N\), all indices satisfy \(n-i\ge n/2\).
Since \(W(n)\le\log n\),
\[
 k_n(n-i)
 \le\left(\frac{2e\log n}{n}\right)^{n/2}
 \le2^{-n/2}
\]
for large \(N\).
The sum over at most \(N\) indices \(i\), then over \(n>2N\),
is exponentially small.
This proves \eqref{bnc:eq:bell-23}.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Completion of the convergence proof}

Since
\[
 y_n-y_{n-1-m}=\sum_{j=0}^{m}\xi_{n-j},
\]
the Poisson equation \eqref{bnc:eq:bell-17} is exactly
\[
 \Xi_n=\sum_{j\ge0}q_{W(n)}(j)\xi_{n-j}.
\]
All nonzero \(\xi_{n-j}\) in this identity have \(j<n\).
Define
\[
 \bar\xi_n=\frac{\Xi_n}{1+W(n)}
     =\sum_{j\ge0}k_n(j)\xi_{n-j}.
\]
Equation \eqref{bnc:eq:bell-17} implies
\begin{equation}\label{bnc:eq:bell-26}
\bar\xi_n=O(W(n)^5/n^2),\qquad \sum_{n\ge1}|\bar\xi_n|<\infty.
\end{equation}
Summing the finite triangular sums for \(1\le n\le N\) gives the
exact identity
\begin{equation}\label{bnc:eq:bell-27}
\sum_{n=1}^{N}\bar\xi_n
 =\sum_{i=1}^{N}\xi_i
       \sum_{j=0}^{N-i}k_{i+j}(j)
 =\sum_{i=1}^{N}\kappa_i \xi_i-B_N.
\end{equation}
By \eqref{bnc:eq:bell-16} and \eqref{bnc:eq:bell-22},
\begin{equation}\label{bnc:eq:bell-28}
\sum_{i\ge1}|(\kappa_i-1)\xi_i|
   <\infty,
 \qquad
 \sum_{i>N}|(\kappa_i-1)\xi_i|
     =O(W(N)^2/N).
\end{equation}
The boundary \(B_N\) tends to zero by \eqref{bnc:eq:bell-23}.
Equations \eqref{bnc:eq:bell-26}--\eqref{bnc:eq:bell-28} show that
\(\sum_{i=1}^{N}\xi_i\) converges.
Since \(y_N=y_0+\sum_{i=1}^{N}\xi_i\), there is a finite real
\(\ell\) such that \(y_N\to\ell\).

More precisely,
\[
 \ell
 =y_0+\sum_{n\ge1}\bar\xi_n
        -\sum_{i\ge1}(\kappa_i-1)\xi_i,
\]
and both displayed series converge absolutely.
The tails in \eqref{bnc:eq:bell-26} and \eqref{bnc:eq:bell-28}, together with \eqref{bnc:eq:bell-23}, give
\[
 y_N=\ell+O(W(N)^5/N).
\]
Exponentiation proves
\[
 a_n=e^\ell\mathcal N_n
       \left(1+O(W(n)^5/n)\right).
\]
Thus \(C_*=e^\ell\) is finite and strictly positive.
The classical Bell saddle equivalent completes the claimed
Bell-number formulation.

\paragraph{Logical dependency.}
The proof uses the inherited root-growth, monotone-ratio, and
logarithmic-window coefficient theorems; the new complementary-endpoint
estimate; explicit differentiation of the proposed normalizer; positive
barriers; and the elementary column-stability argument.
Neither Part~III's unproved local-curvature estimate \eqref{ble:eq:local}
nor its sufficient tilted-variance estimate \eqref{ble:eq:varlemma}
enters the argument.
The existence portion of the finer Bell comparison is thereby proved by
a different route.



\section{The companion sequence A088713 has the same constant}\label{bnc:sec:companion}

Let
\[
 C(z)=\frac1{1-zA(z)}=\sum_{n\ge0}c_nz^n.
\]
Its coefficients are OEIS A088713 \cite{oeisC}:
\[
 1,1,2,6,24,118,674,\ldots.
\]
Part~II proves only
\(\log c_n=\log a_{n-1}+O(\log n)\) (Proposition~\ref{gml:prop:companion}),
and explicitly leaves \(c_n\sim a_{n-1}\) unproved (Research
question~23.6).
The same decomposition by a unique large inner index proves this
equivalent and a full fixed-order endpoint expansion.

\paragraph{A second route.}
The fixed-order expansion in the first display of
Theorem~\ref{bnc:thm:companion} below is Theorem~\ref{gdy:ren:theorem} of
Part~IV for this pair (the coefficients \(\gamma_h\) are those of
\eqref{gdy:ren:d}). Manuscripts~01 and~02 proved it independently. Part~IV's
theorem is more general: it holds for every log-convex sequence with
\(a_n/a_{n-1}\sim n/\log n\), and its consequence \eqref{gdy:ren:first}
gives the first correction \(2\log n/n\). The proof below uses the
composition structure of A088714 and the bound \(c_h\le a_h\); it is
printed as a second proof of that expansion. The normalization of \(c_n\)
with the constant \(C_*\) of Theorem~\ref{bnc:thm:bell}, the last two
displays of the theorem, is new in Part~V.

Put
\[
 C(z)^2=\sum_{h\ge0}\gamma_hz^h.
\]
The first endpoint coefficients are
\[
 \gamma_0=1,\quad \gamma_1=2,\quad \gamma_2=5,\quad \gamma_3=16,\quad
 \gamma_4=64,\quad \gamma_5=308.
\]

\begin{theorem}[Companion endpoint expansion and normalization]\label{bnc:thm:companion}
For every fixed nonnegative integer \(P\),
\[
 \boxed{
 c_n=\sum_{h=0}^{P}\gamma_h a_{n-1-h}
           +O_P(a_{n-P-2}).}
\]
In particular,
\[
 c_n=a_{n-1}+2a_{n-2}+5a_{n-3}+O(a_{n-4}),
 \qquad c_n\sim a_{n-1}.
\]
With the same constant \(C_*\) as in the A088714 normalization theorem,
\[
 \boxed{
 c_n=C_*\frac{W(n)}{n}\mathcal N_n
        \left(1+O\!\left(\frac{W(n)^5}{n}\right)\right).}
\]
Consequently
\[
 c_n\sim C_*\frac{W(n)}{n}
              \mathsf B_n\exp(W(n)^2+3W(n)).
\]
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The formal renewal identity gives
\[
 c_n=\sum_{k=1}^{n}[z^{n-k}]A(z)^k.
\]
Again write \(N=\lfloor n/2\rfloor\), \(H=n-N-2\), and
\(A_N(z)=\sum_{j=0}^{N}a_jz^j\).
Configurations in which every selected inner index is at most \(N\)
contribute
\[
 E_n=[z^n]\frac{zA_N(z)}{1-zA_N(z)}.
\]
At \(z_n=4/R_n\), the finite-polynomial estimate gives
\(A_N(z_n)=1+z_n+O(z_n^2)\), so \(z_nA_N(z_n)<1\).
Nonnegative coefficients therefore yield
\[
 E_n\le z_n^{-n}
       \frac{z_nA_N(z_n)}{1-z_nA_N(z_n)}
       =O(z_n^{-n+1}).
\]
The inherited root growth implies
\(E_n=O(a_{n-1}e^{-\eta n})\) for some fixed \(\eta>0\).
Only the rational function built from the finite polynomial \(A_N\)
has been evaluated; no convergence of \(A\) or \(C\) is assumed.

Any other configuration has one and only one inner index greater than
\(N\), since the sum of all inner indices is at most \(n-1<2(N+1)\).
Write its value as \(n-1-h\), where \(0\le h\le H\).
If the original product had \(k\) factors, the remaining \(k-1\)
inner indices have total degree \(h-(k-1)\), and there are \(k\)
possible locations for the large index.
Thus these configurations contribute exactly
\[
 \sum_{h=0}^{H}a_{n-1-h}
       \sum_{k=1}^{h+1} k[z^{h-k+1}]A(z)^{k-1}.
\]
The inner coefficient equals \(\gamma_h\), because
\[
 \sum_{k\ge1} k(zA(z))^{k-1}
       =\frac1{(1-zA(z))^2}=C(z)^2.
\]
Hence
\begin{equation}\label{bnc:eq:bell-29}
c_n=E_n+\sum_{h=0}^{H}\gamma_h a_{n-1-h}.
\end{equation}

For the tail estimate, first note that the original functional equation
is equivalent to \(A(z)=C(zA(z))\).
Since every coefficient is nonnegative and \(A(0)=1\), this implies
coefficientwise \(C(z)\le A(z)\), or \(c_h\le a_h\).
Log-convexity and \(a_0=1\) give \(a_i a_j\le a_{i+j}\).
Therefore
\[
 \gamma_h=\sum_{i=0}^{h}c_i c_{h-i}
       \le\sum_{i=0}^{h}a_i a_{h-i}
       \le(h+1)a_h.
\]
For fixed \(p\), the same geometric-ratio argument as in the
weighted endpoint-tail lemma gives
\[
 \sum_{h=p}^{H}(h+1)a_h a_{n-1-h}
       =O_p(a_{n-1-p}).
\]
Indeed the consecutive product ratio is
\(R_{h+1}/R_{n-1-h}\); it is at most \(3/4\) up to \(n/3\),
for large \(n\), and at most \(1\) for the rest of the range.
Taking \(p=P+1\) in \eqref{bnc:eq:bell-29} proves the fixed-order expansion.

The first two terms give
\(c_n=a_{n-1}(1+O(W(n)/n))\).
The A088714 theorem gives
\(a_{n-1}=C_*\mathcal N_{n-1}(1+O(W(n)^5/n))\).
Finally the explicit model ratio satisfies
\[
 \mathcal N_{n-1}
   =\frac{W(n)}{n}\mathcal N_n
      (1+O(W(n)/n)).
\]
Combining these three relations proves the companion normalization with
the same constant \(C_*\).
\end{proof}

\section{What has changed mathematically, and the computations of Part~V}\label{bnc:sec:changes}

The source boundary and the new conclusions for A088714 and A088713 are
summarized in Table~\ref{bnc:tab:boundary}. (The manuscript's table also
has two rows on A321941, printed with its part on that sequence.) In this
problem an \(n\)-th-root asymptotic leaves room for multiplicative
factors whose logarithms diverge; the new proofs supply exactly the
missing global control.

\begin{table}[htbp]
\centering\small
\caption{Earlier results of Parts~I--III and the conclusions of Part~V.}
\label{bnc:tab:boundary}
\begin{tabular}{@{}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{23mm}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.38\textwidth}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.38\textwidth}@{}}
\toprule
Topic&Inspected earlier boundary&Part~V\\
\midrule
A088714 endpoint&
The local coefficient expansion is proved; an upper bound for the
complementary endpoint is missing.&
Every fixed-order endpoint expansion, beginning
\(2a_{n-1}+5a_{n-2}+24a_{n-3}\).\\[3pt]
A088714 scale&
\(a_n^{1/n}\sim n/(e\log n)\) and \(R_n\sim n/\log n\);
full Bell normalization is conjectural.&
\(a_n=C_*\mathcal N_n(1+O(W(n)^5/n))\), with
\(C_*\) finite and positive.\\[3pt]
A088713 scale&
\(\log c_n=\log a_{n-1}+O(\log n)\); the equivalent remains open.&
\(c_n\sim a_{n-1}\), a fixed-order expansion, and the same
normalization constant \(C_*\).\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

For A088714 the important distinction is between an explicit
\emph{comparison} sequence and the actual sequence.  Differentiating
\(\log\mathcal N(x)\) is legitimate and gives a smooth model for the
local weights.  The actual ratios are initially used only through
monotonicity and their proved equivalent.  Positive barriers first
bound the normalized sequence; a separate bounded-additive-error
argument then makes linearization valid.  The column-sum identity
converts a summable averaging defect into convergence.  This
separation avoids assuming the sought local regularity in order to
prove the normalization.

[Batch 86, 4 October 2026. The A088713 row overlaps Part~IV: the
fixed-order expansion and \(c_n\sim a_{n-1}\) are also
Theorem~\ref{gdy:ren:theorem} and \eqref{gdy:ren:first}, proved
independently (Section~\ref{bnc:sec:companion}); the normalization of
\(c_n\) with \(C_*\) is Part~V's alone.]

\subsection{What is checked exactly}

The A088714 verification programs use the Python standard library.
The included checks, in
\nolinkurl{code/05-bell-norm-verify_complement.py} with its recorded
output \nolinkurl{data/05-bell-norm-endpoint_verification.json}, cover:
\begin{enumerate}
\item The exact A088714 recurrence, endpoint-coefficient identities,
and \(209\) three-region decompositions through \(n=32\).
\item The companion prefix, its endpoint coefficients, and
\(27\) exact companion decompositions.
\end{enumerate}
(The manuscript's list begins with four items on A321941, checked by the
programs shipped with its part on that sequence; the shared driver
\nolinkurl{code/05-bell-norm-run_checks.py} runs all of them, see the
README.)
The tests are finite certificates and checks of algebraic
identifications.  They do not establish the infinite asymptotic
remainder bounds; those are proved in Sections~\ref{bnc:sec:endpoint}
to~\ref{bnc:sec:companion}.

\subsection{Finite normalization diagnostics}

The A088714 coefficients through \(600\) were generated as exact
integers.  The normalization table uses Decimal arithmetic at precisions
\(80\) and \(120\), each with \(20\) guard digits.
The displayed values agree at the two precisions. The files are:
\begin{itemize}[nosep]
\item the program: \nolinkurl{code/05-bell-norm-normalization_diagnostics.py};
\item the coefficients: \nolinkurl{data/05-bell-norm-coefficients_600.txt};
\item the run at 80 digits: \nolinkurl{data/05-bell-norm-normalization_80.json};
\item the run at 120 digits: \nolinkurl{data/05-bell-norm-normalization_120.json}.
\end{itemize}
The Lambert equation \(we^w=n\) is solved by Newton iteration,
and logarithms are taken only after the integer coefficients have
been computed.

\begin{table}[htbp]

Decimals are rounded; they are not certified intervals for \(C_*\).}
\label{bnc:tab:bellnumeric}
\begin{tabular}{rrrr}
\toprule
\(n\)&\(a_n/\mathcal N_n\)&\(R_n-n/W(n)\)&\(c_n/a_{n-1}\)\\
\midrule
25 &0.032465579673&2.539032789&1.199848754\\
50 &0.038089854602&2.312570363&1.118554273\\

The normalization theorem proves that the normalized sequence has a
positive finite limit.  It does not make the last finite value
in Table~\ref{bnc:tab:bellnumeric} a numerical approximation with a
certified error bound for that limit.  The constants hidden in
the asymptotic error have not been made explicit here.
Likewise, the apparent approach of \(R_n-n/W(n)\) to two is
consistent with the remaining pointwise-ratio conjecture; the
proved statement at that precision is only \(O(1)\).

\section{A proposed further-research program for Part~V}\label{bnc:sec:future}

The following questions are deliberately more precise than a request
for additional numerical terms.  Each identifies a mathematical
target that is not established by the present proofs. (The manuscript's
questions on A321941, its Research questions~15.6--15.8, are printed
with its part on that sequence.)

\begin{question}[An effective value of \(C_*\)]\label{bnc:q:constant}
Can the implicit constants and starting indices in the normalization
proof be made explicit enough to give a certified numerical interval
for \(C_*\)?
\end{question}
The convergence proof already represents \(\log C_*\) as the
difference of two absolutely convergent auxiliary series.
An effective version would bound their tails, together with the
column boundary term, using computable constants.
The finite values in Table~\ref{bnc:tab:bellnumeric} can guide acceleration,
but interval arithmetic and explicit inequalities are needed for
a certified answer.  An expression in familiar constants is a
separate question; numerical recognition alone would not settle it.

\begin{question}[The next term of the actual ratio]\label{bnc:q:ratio}
Does the expansion proposed in Part~I, \eqref{eq:neededratio}, hold in
the form
\[
 R_n=\frac n{W(n)}+2+\frac{W(n)}{2(1+W(n))^2}
          +O\!\left(\frac{W(n)^p}{n}\right)
\]
for some fixed \(p\), and what is the sharp exponent or leading
remainder?
\end{question}
We proved this shape for the explicit model ratio \(s_n\), but
only \(R_n=n/W(n)+O(1)\) for the actual sequence.
The full normalization does not justify differentiating its
unknown error term.  A quantitative smoothing argument for the
normalized recurrence, or control of tilted moment variances,
could supply the missing pointwise information.

\begin{question}[Higher-order Bell corrections]\label{bnc:q:bellhigher}
Is there a complete expansion of
\[
 \log\frac{a_n}{C_*\mathcal N_n}
\]
in powers of \(1/n\), with coefficients that are explicit functions
of \(W(n)\)?
\end{question}
The fixed-order endpoint expansion supplies every required
large-inner-index coefficient.  The local coefficient extraction
can also be continued to higher orders.  The remaining task is to
propagate these expansions through the averaging equation with a
uniform error.  The present \(O(W(n)^5/n)\) bound is sufficient for
convergence and is not claimed to be optimal.

\begin{question}[A general normalization theorem for growing lags]
Which slowly varying lag laws admit a column-sum argument of the
kind used in Section~\ref{bnc:sec:bellconvergence}?
\end{question}
For a general triangular averaging kernel \(k_n(j)\), the exact
summation identity suggests three quantities to control: the
completed column sums, their errors weighted by the increments
of the unknown sequence, and the boundary left by truncating the
triangular sum.  A useful theorem would translate regularity of
the lag law and its mean into checkable bounds for these quantities.
Poisson laws with logarithmic mean are one tractable instance.
This could apply to other self-compositional generating functions
without a separate local-curvature theorem for each sequence.

\begin{question}[Endpoint expansions of growing order]
How far may the truncation order \(P\) grow with \(n\) in the
endpoint formulas for A088714 and A088713?
\end{question}
The current theorems hold for every \emph{fixed} \(P\).
Allowing \(P=P(n)\to\infty\) requires estimates uniform in the
rapidly growing endpoint coefficients \(\delta_h\) and \(\gamma_h\).
One aim is an optimally truncated expansion with a quantified
small remainder.  Another is a limiting description of the
defect left outside the unique large inner index.  Substituting a
positive number into the divergent endpoint series would not
answer either question.

\begin{question}[A formal proof with a small trusted certificate]
Which portions can be formalized first while keeping the
analytic dependency chain explicit?
\end{question}
For Part~V, the coefficientwise majorants and the positive-kernel
summation identity are good initial targets (the manuscript names also
the finite rational certificate and the exact spectral bridge of its
part on A321941). A useful staged formalization would
state the analytic inputs as explicit theorems, verify every
finite truncation algebraically, and then discharge the limiting
estimates.  This would also make the distinction between formal
series identities and convergent analytic identities mechanically
visible.

\clearpage
\appendix
\noindent\emph{Appendix~\ref{gml:app:audit} belongs to Part~II; it follows Parts~III--V only because the appendix closes the report.}
\section{Dependency and novelty audit}\label{gml:app:audit}
The main logical dependencies are as follows. The formal equation and
the two compact positive-operator constructions prove existence of a
limiting moment measure. The inherited growth bound supplies exponential
integrability and determinacy, and also rules out bounded support.
Moment positivity proves the strict Hankel and log-convexity statements.
Weak convergence of compact approximants proves the real functional
equation. Its inverse form, independently of any ratio asymptotics,
proves the golden-ratio transform law. The self-contained Tauberian lemma
then proves the small-$t$ distribution-function laws. Separately,
log-convexity plus the inherited growth bound proves the consecutive
ratios, and these prove the far tails by size-biasing.

This separation avoids several invalid shortcuts: no divergent ordinary
series is substituted at a positive real argument; no neighboring ratio
is deduced just from an $n$th-root equivalent; no infinite zero or
positivity statement is inferred from finite determinants; no cumulative
mass asymptotic is differentiated to invent a density; and no numerical
agreement is used to exchange the compact-approximation and large-argument
limits.
